Classification of kink clusters for scalar fields in dimension 1+1 Thanks: J. Jendrej is supported by ANR-18-CE40-0028 project “ESSED” and ERC Starting Grant “INSOLIT” 101117126. A. Lawrie is supported by NSF grant DMS-2247290.
Jacek Jendrej, Andrew Lawrie

Abstract
. We consider a real scalar field equation in dimension 1 + 1 1+1 with an even, positive self-interaction potential having two non-degenerate zeros (vacua) 1 1 and − 1 -1 . Such a model admits non-trivial static solutions called kinks and antikinks. We define a kink n n -cluster to be a solution approaching, for large positive times, a superposition of n n alternating kinks and antikinks whose velocities converge to 0 0 . They can be equivalently characterized as the solutions of minimal possible energy containing n − 1 n-1 transitions between the vacua, or as the solutions whose kinetic energy decays to 0 0 in large time. Our first main result is a determination of the main-order asymptotic behavior of any kink n n -cluster. The proof relies on a reduction, using appropriately chosen modulation parameters, to an n n -body problem with attractive exponential interactions. We then construct a kink n n -cluster for any prescribed initial positions of the kinks and antikinks, provided that their mutual distances are sufficiently large. Next, we prove that the set of all the kink n n -clusters is an n n -dimensional topological manifold, and we show how it can be parametrized by the positions of the kinks in the configuration. The proof relies on energy estimates and the contraction mapping principle, using the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction technique. Finally, we show that kink clusters are universal profiles for the formation/collapse of multikink configurations. In this sense, they can be interpreted as forming the stable/unstable manifold of the multikink state given by a superposition of n n infinitely separated alternating kinks and antikinks.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35L71 (primary), 35B40, 37K40

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Setting of the problem

We study scalar field equations in dimension
1+1
, which are associated to the Lagrangian action

(1.1)

\mathscr{L}(\phi)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}-U(\phi)\Big)\,\mathrm{\,d}x\mathrm{\,d}t,

where the self-interaction potential
U:\mathbb{R}\to[0,+\infty)
is a given smooth function.
The unknown field
\phi=\phi(t,x)
is assumed to be real-valued.
The resulting Euler-Lagrange equation is

(1.2)

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(t,x)+U^{\prime}(\phi(t,x))=0,\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},\ \phi(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}.

We assume that

•

U
is an even function,

•

U(\phi)>0
for all
\phi\in(-1,1)
,

•

U(-1)=U(1)=0
and
U^{\prime\prime}(1)=U^{\prime\prime}(-1)=1
.

The zeros of
U
are called the
vacua
.
Linearization of (
1.2
) around each of the vacua

1
and
-1
,
\phi=\pm 1+g
, yields the free linear Klein-Gordon equation of mass
1
:

(1.3)

\partial_{t}^{2}g_{\mathrm{L}}(t,x)-\partial_{x}^{2}g_{\mathrm{L}}(t,x)+g_{\mathrm{L}}(t,x)=0.

Two well-known examples of (
1.2
) satisfying the hypotheses above are the
sine-Gordon equation

(1.4)

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\frac{1}{\pi}\sin(\pi\phi(t,x))=0,

where we have taken
U(\phi)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\big(1+\cos(\pi\phi)\big)
, and the
\phi^{4}
model

(1.5)

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}\phi(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\phi(t,x)^{3}=0,

for which
U(\phi)=\frac{1}{8}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
.

The equation (
1.2
) can be rewritten as a system of first order in
t
:

(1.6)

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\phi(t,x)\\
\dot{\phi}(t,x)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\phi}(t,x)\\
\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(t,x)-U^{\prime}(\phi(t,x))\end{pmatrix}.

We denote
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}=(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})^{\mathrm{T}}
an element of the phase space
(in the sequel, we omit the transpose in the notation).
The potential energy
E_{p}
, the kinetic energy
E_{k}
and the total energy
E

of a state are given by

(1.7)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{p}}(\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+U(\phi_{0}(x))\Big)\mathrm{\,d}x,

(1.8)

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{k}}(\dot{\phi}_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{2}(\dot{\phi}_{0}(x))^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x,

(1.9)

\displaystyle E(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\dot{\phi}_{0}(x))^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+U(\phi_{0}(x))\Big)\mathrm{\,d}x.

Denoting
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t,x)\coloneqq(\phi(t,x),\dot{\phi}(t,x))
, the system (
1.6
) can be reformulated in the Hamiltonian form as

(1.10)

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)),

where
\boldsymbol{J}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}
is the standard symplectic form
and
\mathrm{D}
is the Fréchet derivative for the
L^{2}\times L^{2}
inner product.
In particular,
E
is a conserved quantity, and we denote
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})
the energy of a solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
of (
1.10
).

The set of finite energy states
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}=(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})
contains the following affine spaces:

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1,1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0}):E(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})<\infty\ \text{and}\ \lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=1,\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-1,-1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0}):E(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})<\infty\ \text{and}\ \lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=-1,\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0}):E(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})<\infty\ \text{and}\ \lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=1,\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-1,1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0}):E(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})<\infty\ \text{and}\ \lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=-1,\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{0}(x)=1\}.

In the case of the
\phi^{4}
model, these are all the finite-energy states,
but in general there can be other states of finite energy,
for example if
U
has other vacua than
1
and
-1
.
Here, the states which we consider will always belong to one of the four affine spaces listed above, which are all parallel to the space

(1.12)

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}\coloneqq H^{1}\times L^{2}.\end{split}

Equation (
1.10
) admits static solutions. They are the critical points
of the potential energy. The trivial ones are the vacuum fields
\phi(t,x)=\pm 1
.
The solution
\phi(t,x)=1
(resp.
\phi(t,x)=-1
) has zero energy and is the ground state in
\mathcal{E}_{1,1}

(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{-1,-1}
).

There are also non-constant static solutions
\phi(t,x)

connecting the two vacua, that is

(1.13)

\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi(t,x)=\mp 1,\quad\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi(t,x)=\pm 1.

One can describe all these solutions.
There exists a unique increasing odd function
H:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)
such that
all the solutions of (
1.13
) are given by
\phi(t,x)=\pm H(x-a)
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
The basic properties of
H
are given in Section
2
.
Its translates are called the
kinks
and are the ground states in
\mathcal{E}_{-1,1}
.
The translates of the function
-H
are called the
antikinks
and are the ground states in
\mathcal{E}_{1,-1}
.

The condition (
1.13
) defines a
topological class
, since for any continuous path of finite energy states, either all or none of them satisfy (
1.13
). In general, minimizers of the energy in a topological class that does not contain vacua are called topological solitons. Topological solitons were introduced in the physics literature by Skyrme as candidates for particles in classical field theories; see
[
62
,
41
]
. Kinks and antikinks are one dimensional examples, and in higher dimensions examples include vortices, harmonic maps, monopoles, Skyrmions, and instantons. In this context, it is natural to investigate to what extent multikinks (defined below) can be effectively described as a system of interacting point particles. In other words, can their dynamical behavior be captured by means of a small number of parameters, for instance their positions and momenta.

The Lagrangian (
1.1
) is invariant under the Poincaré group, from which we see that applying a Lorentz boost to the stationary kink

\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=(H(x-a),0)
, one obtains a traveling kink.
Given
(a,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times(-1,1)
, we denote

(1.14)

H(a,v;x)\coloneqq H(\gamma_{v}(x-a)),\qquad\gamma_{v}\coloneqq(1-v^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

and

(1.15)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}(a,v;x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(H(a,v;x),-v\partial_{x}H(a,v;x)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(H(\gamma_{v}(x-a)),-v\gamma_{v}\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}(x-a))\big).

Then
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{H}(a+vt,v)
is a solution of (
1.10
),
in other words

(1.16)

-v\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)=v\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)=\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)).

The
momentum
of a state
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}=(\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0})

is given by

(1.17)

P(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0})=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dot{\phi}_{0}(x)\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x)\mathrm{\,d}x=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0},\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\rangle.

It is well defined for every state of finite energy and is a conserved quantity. Using (
2.21
),
it is easy to compute that

(1.18)

\displaystyle E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))

\displaystyle=\gamma_{v}M,

(1.19)

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))

\displaystyle=\gamma_{v}Mv.

A traveling kink is thus similar to a relativistic point particle
of mass
M
and velocity
v
, and the formulas above
are the usual formulas of Special Relativity.

1.2.
The notion of a kink cluster

By the variational characterization of
H
and its translates as the ground states in
\mathcal{E}_{-1,1}
,
one can view them as the transitions between the two vacua
-1
and
1

having the minimal possible energy
E=E_{p}(H)
.
Given a natural number
n
, we are interested in solutions of (
1.2
) containing,
asymptotically as
t\to\infty
,
n
such transitions.
Since energy
E_{p}(H)
is needed for each transition, we necessarily have
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\geq nE_{p}(H)
. We call
kink clusters
the solutions for which equality holds.

Definition 1.1

(Kink
n
-cluster)
.

Let
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
.
We say that a solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
of (
1.10
) is a
kink
n
-cluster
if
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\leq nE_{p}(H)
and there exist real-valued functions

x_{0}(t)\leq x_{1}(t)\leq\ldots\leq x_{n}(t)
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi(t,x_{k}(t))=(-1)^{k}\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}.

Note that the kink
0
-clusters are the constant solutions
\phi\equiv 1
and the kink
1
-clusters are the antikinks.
We say that
\phi
is a kink cluster if it is a kink
n
-cluster for some
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
.

From the heuristic discussion above, the shape of each transition
in a kink cluster has to be close to optimal,
that is close to a kink or an antikink.
Before we give a precise statement of this fact,
we introduce the so-called
multikink configurations
.

Let
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the positions of the transitions
and
\vec{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in(-1,1)^{n}
the Lorentz parameters
(following
[
41
, Chapter 5]
, we use the letter

a
for the translation parameter; it should not be confused
with “acceleration” which will be given no symbol in this paper).
We always assume
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}
.
It will be convenient to abbreviate
\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{H}(a_{k},v_{k})
,

\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\coloneqq\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a_{k},v_{k})
,
\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\coloneqq\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a_{k},v_{k})

for
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We also denote
\boldsymbol{1}\coloneqq(1,0)
and

(1.20)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v};x)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(x)+\boldsymbol{1}).

For example, if
n=0
, then
\vec{a},\vec{v}
are empty vectors and
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
is the vacuum
\boldsymbol{1}
.
If
n=1
,
\vec{a}=(a_{1})
and
\vec{v}=(v_{1})
, then
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=-\boldsymbol{H}(a_{1},v_{1})
is an antikink.
If
n=2
,
\vec{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})
with
a_{2}-a_{1}\gg 1
and
\vec{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})
, then

\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=\boldsymbol{1}-\boldsymbol{H}(a_{1},v_{1})+\boldsymbol{H}(a_{2},v_{2})
has the shape of an antikink near
x=a_{1}
,
and of a kink near
x=a_{2}
. These are the
kink-antikink pairs
, which we studied with Kowalczyk in
[
26
]
.

Employing the term introduced by Martel and Raphaël in
[
47
]
,
we study multikinks in the regime of
strong interactions
, so we will always assume that Lorentz parameters
\vec{v}
are small, and the positions of the kinks
\vec{a}
are well-separated. We weigh these requirements as follows.

Definition 1.2

(Weight of modulation parameters)
.

For all
(\vec{a},\vec{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we set

(1.21)

\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{-(a_{k+1}-a_{k})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}^{2}.

With these notations we define a distance to the family of
n
-kink configurations. Given
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}
, let

(1.22)

\begin{split}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi})\coloneqq\inf_{(\vec{a},\vec{v})}\Big(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\Big).\end{split}

Proposition 1.3
.

A solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
of (
1.10
) is a kink
n
-cluster if and only if

(1.23)

\lim_{t\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))=0.

In other words, kink clusters can be equivalently defined as solutions approaching,
as
t\to\infty
, a superposition of a finite number of alternating kinks and antikinks,
whose mutual distances tend to
\infty
and which travel with speeds converging to
0
.
In contrast to multikink solutions consisting of Lorentz-boosted kinks (traveling
with asymptotically non-zero speed) constructed in
[
4
]
,
the dynamics of kink clusters are driven solely by interactions between the kinks and antikinks – this is the regime of
strong interactions
.
Proposition
1.3
is proved in Section
5.1
.
It implies in particular that the energy of a kink
n
-cluster equals
nE_{p}(H)
.

In Section
5.2
, we provide another characterization of kink clusters, namely as
asymptotically
static solutions
, by which we mean solutions whose kinetic energy
converges to
0
as
t\to\infty
. In celestial mechanics such solutions are referred to as the
parabolic motions
of the system, see for instance
[
1
, Section 2.4]
and
[
40
,
61
]
.
For simplicity, we restrict our attention
to the
\phi^{4}
self-interaction potential
U(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{1}{8}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
U(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{1}{8}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
.
A solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
of (
1.10
) satisfies
\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\phi}
or

-\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink cluster.

Given a solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)
of (
1.10
) that is close to an
n
-kink configuration at time
t
, i.e., with
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
sufficiently small, we can decompose it as

(1.24)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\boldsymbol{h}(t)\end{split}

with
\|\boldsymbol{h}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))\lesssim{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
.
This decomposition is clearly not unique, but we will make a specific choice of
uniquely determined parameters
enforcing a convenient choice of orthogonality conditions, given by the following standard lemma, which is proved in Lemma
4.1
in Section
4
. We denote

(1.25)

\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{J}\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v),\qquad\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{J}\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v),

and use the shorthand
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{k},v_{k})
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{k},v_{k})
.

Lemma 1.5

(Modulation parameters)
.

There exist
\eta_{0},\eta_{1},C_{0}>0
with the following properties. For all
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}
such that

(1.26)

\begin{split}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0})<\eta_{0},\end{split}

there exist unique
(\vec{a},\vec{v})=(\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}),\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dots\leq a_{n}
such that

(1.27)

\begin{split}\big\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\big\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})<\eta_{1},\end{split}

and

(1.28)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle&=0,\end{split}

for all
k=1,\dots,n
. The pair
(\vec{a},\vec{v})
satisfies the estimates

(1.29)

\begin{split}\big\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\big\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}&+\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})<C_{0}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}).\end{split}

Finally, the map
\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}\owns\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\mapsto(\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}),\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is of class
C^{1}
.

We will refer to
a_{k}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
as the
position
of the
k
th kink and
v_{k}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
as its
velocity
at time
t
.

1.3.
Main results

By Proposition
1.3
and Lemma
1.5
, for any kink
n
-cluster
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, the modulation parameters
(\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)),\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)))
are defined for all sufficiently large times. The first main result, which is is crucial to the other conclusions in this section, is the determination
of the leading order dynamics of the positions and velocities of the kinks.

Define the number
\kappa>0
by

(1.30)

\begin{split}\kappa\coloneqq\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}-\frac{1}{1-y}\Big)\,\mathrm{\,d}y\Big).\end{split}

Theorem 1

(Asymptotic dynamics of kink
n
-clusters)
.

Let
\kappa>0
be given by (
1.30
)
and
M\coloneqq E_{p}(H)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}
be a kink
n
-cluster and let
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))=(\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)),\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
be the positions and velocities of the kinks given by Lemma
1.5
. Then,

(1.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigg(

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k<n}\bigg|\big(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t)\big)-\Big(2\log(\kappa t)-\log\frac{Mk(n-k)}{2}\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq n}|tv_{k}(t)+(n+1-2k)|+t\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))\|_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}\bigg)=0.

Our next result concerns the problem of existence of kink
n
-clusters.
We prove that, for any choice of
n
points on the line sufficiently distant from each other,
there exists a kink
n
-cluster such that the initial positions of the (anti)kinks
are given by the
n
chosen points.
The result is inspired by the work
of Maderna and Venturelli
[
40
]
on the Newtonian
n
-body problem.

Theorem 2

(Existence of kink
n
-clusters)
.

There exist
C_{0},L_{0}>0
such that the following is true.
If
L\geq L_{0}
and
\vec{a}_{0}=(a_{0,1},\dots,a_{0,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies
a_{0,k+1}-a_{0,k}\geq L
for all

k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, then there exists
a solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)
of (
1.10
) satisfying:

•

{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))\leq C_{0}/(e^{L}+t^{2})
for all
t\geq 0
; in particular,
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink
n
-cluster;

•

\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(0))=\vec{a}_{0}
, where
\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(0))
is given by Lemma
1.5
.

Remark 1.6
.

In the case of the sine-Gordon equation (
1.4
), which is completely integrable,
it is in principle possible to obtain explicit kink clusters.
For
n\in\{2,3\}
, such examples of kink clusters were given in
[
42
]
. Nevertheless, Theorems
1

and
2
are new even for the sine-Gordon equation.

We expect that for given widely separated initial positions
\vec{a}_{0}
, there is actually a
unique

choice of
\vec{v}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{0}
(small respectively in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{E}
) which leads to a kink
n
-cluster.
This is clearly true for
n=1
, since in this case we necessarily have
\vec{v}_{0}=\boldsymbol{g}_{0}=0
. For
n=2
,
uniqueness of
\boldsymbol{g}_{0}
can be obtained as
a consequence of our work with Kowalczyk
[
26
]
.
Our next result shows that uniqueness does hold for certain initial position vectors
\vec{a}_{0}
. Indeed, we prove that kink
n
-clusters can be parameterized uniquely (and continuously) by
only the positions
of the kinks, at least for initial configurations that are sufficiently close to the leading order asymptotics from Theorem
1
.

Theorem 3

(Classification of kink
n
-clusters)
.

There exist
\tau_{0},\eta_{0}>0
such that for each
t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
and each
\vec{a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

(1.32)

\begin{split}\max_{1\leq k<n}\bigg|\big(a_{k+1,0}-a_{k,0}\big)-\Big(2\log(\kappa t_{0})-\log\frac{Mk(n-k)}{2}\Big)\bigg|<\eta_{0},\end{split}

there exists a
unique
solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)
of (
1.10
) satisfying:

•

{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t))<\eta_{0}
for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t))=0
;

•

\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(a_{0},t_{0};t_{0})=\vec{a}_{0}
.

Remark 1.7
.

By Theorem
1
, the trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
associated to every kink
n
-cluster
\boldsymbol{\phi}
eventually satisfy (
1.32
), meaning that for all
t_{0}
sufficiently large (depending on
\boldsymbol{\phi}
),
\vec{a}_{0}=\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{0}))
satisfies (
1.32
).

As a by product of the proof of Theorem
3
, we obtain the following refined information about the dynamics of
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)
.

Corollary 1

(Refined dynamics)
.

Let
\tau_{0},\eta_{0}
be as in Theorem
3
,
t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
, let
\vec{a}_{0}
be as in (
1.32
), let
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0})
be the kink
n
-cluster given by Theorem
3
, and let
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))=(\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)),\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t))
. Then

(1.33)

\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}\Big(t^{\gamma}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))\|_{\mathcal{E}}\Big)=0\end{split}

for each
\gamma<2
.
In addition to (
1.31
), the parameters
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
satisfy

(1.34)

\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{2}\Big(|\vec{a}\,^{\prime}(t)-\vec{v}(t)|+|\vec{v}\,^{\prime}(t)|\Big)<\infty.\end{split}

The kink
n
-clusters can be interpreted as forming the stable manifold of the asymptotic state given by infinitely separated multikink configurations. (Note that the unstable manifold can be obtained by applying the time reversal symmetry.) This topological manifold is
n
-dimensional and can be parameterized by the positions of the kinks. We formulate this as follows.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}
be the set of all the possible initial conditions leading to a kink
n
-cluster. In other words,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{n}
if and only if the corresponding solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)
of (
1.10
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{\phi}(0)=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}
is a kink
n
-cluster. Clearly,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is an invariant set.

We fix
\eta_{0},\tau_{0}
as in Theorem
3
and define an open subset
\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

(1.35)

\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})\coloneqq\Big\{\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\exists\,t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}\,\,\textrm{such that}\\
\max_{1\leq k<n}\bigg|\big(a_{k+1,0}-a_{k,0}\big)-\Big(2\log(\kappa t_{0})-\log\frac{Mk(n-k)}{2}\Big)\bigg|<\eta_{0}\Big\}.

By Theorem
3
, to each
\vec{a}_{0}\in\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})
and each
t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
such that (
1.32
) is satisfied we can associate an element of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
via the mapping

(1.36)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Phi}:\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})\to\mathcal{M}_{n};\quad\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})\ni\vec{a}_{0}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\vec{a}_{0})=\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}.\end{split}

Remark 1.8
.

Observe that for a fixed
\vec{a}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, once (
1.32
) is satisfied for some
t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
and
\eta_{0}
as in Theorem
3
, it will also be satisfied for nearby
\widetilde{t}_{0}
. However, it will be evident from the proof that the trajectories
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},\widetilde{t}_{0};t)
are time translations of each other, meaning, for example, if
t_{0}<\widetilde{t}_{0}
we have
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t)=\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},\widetilde{t}_{0};t+(\widetilde{t}_{0}-t_{0}))
.
It follows that the map
\Phi
is independent of the choice of
t_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
such that (
1.32
) holds. Indeed, for a fixed
\vec{a}_{0}\in\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})
, different choices
t_{0},\widetilde{t}_{0}\geq\tau_{0}
such that (
1.32
) holds are associated to the same element of the phase space, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},t_{0};t_{0})=\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0},\widetilde{t}_{0};\widetilde{t}_{0})
. In other words, the mapping (
1.36
) associates to each element
\vec{a}_{0}\in\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0})
a unique element of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, which we can denote more succinctly by
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\vec{a}_{0})
.

Corollary 2

(Kink
n
-clusters as an invariant topological manifold)
.

The set
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is an
n
-dimensional topological manifold. The mapping (
1.36
) is a homeomorphism onto its image
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0}))
, which we denote by
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\mathcal{T}(\eta_{0}))=:\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{n}
. Moreover, for each trajectory
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)\subset\mathcal{M}_{n}
there exists a time
t_{0}>0
such that
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)\in\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Remark 1.9
.

We believe that
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a
smooth

n
-dimensional invariant manifold, but we do not pursue its regularity here. We believe the submanifold
\mathcal{M}_{n}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{E}\mid{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi})<\epsilon\}
(note that this set contains
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
) can also be parameterized uniquely by the positions of the kinks via the map
\boldsymbol{\phi}\mapsto\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi})
, (provided
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small so that this map can be defined via Lemma
1.5
). To prove this we would need to rule out self intersections, i.e., show there are no elements
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\sharp}
of
\mathcal{M}_{n}\cap\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{E}\mid{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi})<\epsilon\}
for which
\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi})=\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\sharp})
but
\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi})\neq\vec{v}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\sharp})
, which we do not accomplish here. Rather we show this non-self intersection property holds only within
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
, i.e., for kink
n
-clusters that are close enough to their asymptotic configurations as in (
1.32
).

The manifold
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and its submanifold
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
play natural roles in the sense of dynamical systems. If we extend the phase space by a state
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{n}
corresponding to the limit of
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
as
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\to 0
, the function
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi})
gives a distance to
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{n}
and the trajectories
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
satisfy
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))=0
, in other words
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is the
stable manifold
of
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{n}
. Since every kink
n
-cluster enters and then never leaves the submanifold
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
after some finite time,
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
forms the
local stable manifold
of
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{n}
.
The (local) unstable manifold can be obtained by applying the time reversal symmetry.

A typical feature of the stable/unstable manifold of a stationary state is that it governs the behavior of trajectories that enter/exit its small neighborhood,
in the sense that such a trajectory, while still/already far away from the stationary state, must be close to its stable/unstable manifold.
(For hyperbolic stationary states, this property is a consequence
of the Hartman-Grobman theorem.)
In the next theorem, we show that the manifold of kink clusters discussed above
plays an analogous role for the formation/collapse of multikink configuration.

Theorem 4

(Universal profiles of multikink formation)
.

Let
\eta>0
be sufficiently small and let
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
be a sequence of solutions of (
1.10
) defined on time intervals
[0,T_{m}]

satisfying the following assumptions:

(1)

\lim_{m\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(T_{m}))=0
,

(2)

{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t))\leq\eta
for all
t\in[0,T_{m}]
,

(3)

{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(0))=\eta
.

Then, after extraction of a subsequence, there exist
0=n^{(0)}<n^{(1)}<\ldots<n^{(\ell)}=n
,
finite energy states
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{(1)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{(\ell)}
and sequences of real numbers
(X_{m}^{(1)})_{m},\ldots,(X_{m}^{(\ell)})_{m}
such that

(1)

for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, the solution
\boldsymbol{P}^{(j)}
of (
1.10
)
for the initial data
\boldsymbol{P}^{(j)}(0)=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{(j)}
is a cluster of
n^{(j)}-n^{(j-1)}
kinks,

(2)

for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell-1\}
,
\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(X_{m}^{(j+1)}-X_{m}^{(j)}\big)=\infty
,

(3)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\Big\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(0)-\Big(\boldsymbol{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{n^{(j-1)}}\big(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{(j)}(\cdot-X_{m}^{(j)})-\boldsymbol{1}\big)\Big)\Big\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0.

The analogy with stable manifolds is most easily understood in the case
n=2
, as Theorem
4
characterizes trajectories in the phase space that enter a small neighborhood of the asymptotic state
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{2}
, by affirming that a such a trajectory,
while still far away from
\boldsymbol{H}^{\infty}_{2}
,
must be close to the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.
In our setting, nonlinear soliton interactions play an analogous role
as exponential (in)stability in the hyperbolic case.

The analogy described above carries over to
n>2
, but is slightly more complicated, since at the “exit” time
t=0
the solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(0)
is close to a superposition of well-separated kink clusters, rather than to a single one. Intuitively, for
n>2
it can happen
that only some of the neighboring kinks “collapse”, while
the distances between other neighboring kinks remain large.

1.4.
Structure of the paper and main ideas

In Section
2
, we recall the basic properties of the stationary solutions
and compute the first non-trivial term in the asymptotic expansion
of the potential energy of a multikink configuration,
E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))
,
as the distances between the kinks tend to infinity.

Section
3
is devoted to a brief presentation of the well-posedness theory
of the equation (
1.10
).

In Section
4
, we implement the
modulation method
,
also called the “method of collective coordinates” in the physics literature. Every kink
n
-cluster asymptotically decomposes into the form

(1.37)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\boldsymbol{g}(t)\end{split}

where
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
are trajectories related to
\boldsymbol{h}(t)
by the orthogonality conditions (
1.28
) and satisfies

(1.38)

\begin{split}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))\to 0{\ \ \text{as}\ \ }t\to\infty,\end{split}

by Propositon
1.3
.
The idea is to rewrite (
1.10
) as a coupled system of equations for
the
modulation parameters

\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)
and the
remainder

\boldsymbol{g}(t)
. Indeed, using Lemma
1.5
we see that (
1.10
) can be converted to a system for
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t),\boldsymbol{g}(t))\in C^{1}([t_{0};\infty);\mathbb{R}^{2n})\times C([t_{0},\infty);\mathcal{E})

(1.39)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g}(t)&=\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\boldsymbol{g}(t))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\big(a_{k}^{\prime}(t)\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(t)+v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\partial_{v_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(t)\big)\\
0&=\langle\boldsymbol{g}(t),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}(t)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{g}(t),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}(t)\rangle,\quad\,\forall k\in\{1,\dots,n\}.\end{split}

To obtain the equations for the trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
we can pair the equation above with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}(t)
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}(t)
while using the identities

(1.40)

\begin{split}a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle+v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle&=\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle\\
a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle+v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle&=\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{k},v_{k})\rangle,\end{split}

which are obtained by differentiation in time of the second line in the coupled system (
1.39
).

Rather than write down the nonlinear system for
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
explicitly here, we instead build intuition for it as follows. Let us drop for a moment the Lorentz parameters from the ansatz, and introduce the notation
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a})\coloneqq\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{0})
. If we consider an isolated system of
n
points on the real line, located at
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, whose masses are equal to
M>0
and the total potential energy of the system is given by
E_{\mathrm{p}}(H(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))
,
then the principles of Newtonian mechanics assert that the positions of the masses evolve according to the system of differential equations

(1.41)

a_{k}^{\prime}(t)=M^{-1}p_{k}(t),\qquad p_{k}^{\prime}(t)=F_{k}(a_{1}(t),\ldots,a_{n}(t))=-\partial_{a_{k}}E_{\mathrm{p}}(H(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})).

The main conclusion of Section
4
is Lemma
4.7
,
which states that the modulation parameters of a kink cluster

approximately
satisfy this system of ODEs with
M\vec{v}(t)=\vec{p}(t)
.
This step relies on appropriately defined
localized momenta
,
a method first used in
[
23
]
in a similar context,
and inspired by
[
59
, Proposition 4.3]
.

After replacing
E_{\mathrm{p}}(H(\vec{a}))
by its leading term computed in Section
2
,
the system (
1.41
) becomes an
n
-body problem with
attractive exponential
nearest-neighbour interactions

(1.42)

a_{k}^{\prime}(t)=M^{-1}p_{k}(t),\qquad p_{k}^{\prime}(t)=2\kappa^{2}\big(e^{-(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t))}-e^{-(a_{k}(t)-a_{k-1}(t))}\big),

where by convention
a_{0}(t)\coloneqq-\infty
and
a_{n+1}(t)\coloneqq\infty
.
In the case of repulsive interactions, we would have the well-known
Toda system
introduced in
[
64
]
.
Section
6
is devoted to the study of the long-time behaviour of
solutions of this
n
-body problem. The solutions corresponding to kink clusters
are the ones for which the distances between the masses tend to
\infty

and their momenta tend to
0
as
t\to\infty
.
We recall that such solutions are called
parabolic motions
.
One can check that (
1.42
) has an exact solution
(defined up to translation, the mass center being chosen arbitrarily)

(1.43)

a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t)=2\log(\kappa t)-\log\frac{Mk(n-k)}{2},\qquad p_{k}(t)={-}M\frac{n+1-2k}{t}.

In Section
6
, we prove that the leading order
of the asymptotic behavior of
a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t)
and
v_{k}(t)
for any parabolic motion
coincides with (
1.43
) with
M\vec{v}(t)=\vec{p}(t)
.
Our analysis is sufficiently robust to be valid also in the presence of
the error terms obtained from Lemma
4.7
,
which leads to a proof of Theorem
1
.

Remark 1.10
.

Note that (
1.23
) is equivalent to

(1.44)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\inf_{\vec{a}}\Big(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{0})\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a},\vec{0})\Big)=0.

In other words, as long as we are only interested in the (qualitative) smallness of
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi})
,
we can assume without loss of generality that
\vec{v}=0
. For this reason, the expressions
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{0})
and
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{0})
will appear many times.
For the sake of brevity, throughout the paper we will denote

(1.45)

H(\vec{a})\coloneqq H(\vec{a},\vec{0}),\qquad\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a})\coloneqq(H(\vec{a}),0)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{0}),\qquad\rho(\vec{a})\coloneqq\rho(\vec{a},\vec{0}),

and similar doubling of notation will be introduced for a few other objects.

We are aware that this simultaneous use of the “relativistic” multikink configurations
H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
and their “non-relativistic” version
H(\vec{a}(t))
is an unpleasant feature of the paper,
but it also reflects a fundamental conceptual problem. Namely, on one hand we deal with a relativistic equation (
1.2
), which favors the use of the relativistic version. On the other hand, the
n
-body problem with instantaneously acting forces, like (
1.42
), is a purely non-relativistic concept, hence in many parts of the paper the non-relativistic version is better suited.

Remark 1.11
.

Let us mention that Hénon
[
20
]
found
n
independent conserved quantities
for the Toda system (both in the repulsive and in the attractive case).
For parabolic motions, all these quantities are equal to
0
,
which allows to reduce the problem to a system of
n
equations of 1st order.
Probably, this approach could lead to some simplifications in determining
the asymptotic behavior of the parabolic motions of the system (
1.42
),
and perhaps also of the approximate system satisfied by the modulation parameters.
Our arguments do not explicitly rely on the conservation laws related to the complete
integrability of the Toda system, and we expect that part of the analysis
will be applicable also in the cases where the modulation equations are not related
to any completely integrable system of ODEs.

Theorem
2
is proved in Section
7
.
The overall proof scheme is taken from Martel
[
43
]
,
see also the earlier work of Merle
[
48
]
, and contains two steps:

•

for any
T>0
, prove existence of a solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
satisfying
the conclusions of Theorem
2
, but only on the finite time interval
t\in[0,T]
,

•

take a sequence
T_{m}\to\infty
and consider a weak limit
of the solutions
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
obtained in the first step with
T=T_{m}
.

The first step relies on a novel application of the Poincaré-Miranda theorem,
which is essentially a version of Brouwer’s fixed point theorem.
We choose data close to a multikink configuration at time
t=T
and control
how it evolves backwards in time.
It could happen that the multikink collapses before reaching the time
t=0
.
For this reason, we introduce an appropriately defined “exit time”
T_{1}
.
The mapping which assigns the positions of the (anti)kinks at time
T_{1}

to their positions at time
T
turns out to be continuous and, for topological reasons,
surjective in the sense required by Theorem
2
.

In the second step, it is crucial to dispose of some
uniform
estimate on the sequence
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
. In our case, the relevant inequality is
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t))\lesssim(e^{L}+t^{2})^{-1}
with a universal constant.
The existence of such a uniform bound is related to what we would call the “ejection property”
of the system. Intuitively, once
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
starts to grow,
it has to continue growing at a definite rate until the multikink configuration collapses.

In Section
8
, we give a proof of Theorem
4
.
The identification of the clusters presents no difficulty: the positions of any two consecutive (anti)kinks at time
t=0
, after taking a subsequence in
m
, either remain at a bounded distance
or separate with their distance growing to infinity as
m\to\infty
.
This dichotomy determines whether
they fall into the same cluster or to distinct ones.
The next step is to again make use of the ejection property in order to obtain
bounds on
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
independent of
m
, for any
t\geq 0
.
By standard localization techniques involving the finite speed of propagation,
these bounds are inherited by each of the clusters.
We mention that the proof of strong convergence in Theorem
4
(iii)
is based on a novel application of the well-known principle from the Calculus of Variations
affirming that, for a strictly convex functional
\mathcal{F}
, if
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{g}

and
\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{g}_{m})\to\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{g})
, then
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\to\boldsymbol{g}
.

The proof of Theorem
3
, which spans Sections
9
–
12
, is a generalization, refinement, and simplification of the method introduced by the authors with Kowalczyk in
[
26
]
, where we studied the case of kink-antikink pairs, i.e.,
n=2
, and is based on a Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction implemented in our context in the following way.

Fix
\vec{a}_{0}
as in (
1.32
) with
t_{0}
large, and consider the set of all
C^{1}
trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
with
\vec{a}(t_{0})=\vec{a}_{0}
that satisfy

(1.46)

\begin{split}|\vec{a}\,^{\prime}(t)-\vec{v}(t)|+|\vec{v}\,^{\prime}(t)|+|\vec{v}(t)|^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\exp({-(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t))})\lesssim t^{-2}.\end{split}

For each given choice of trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
satisfying (
1.46
) we solve the
projected equation
for the triple
(\boldsymbol{h}(t),\vec{\lambda}(t),\vec{\mu}(t))

(1.47)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{h}(t)&=\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\boldsymbol{h}(t))+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\big(\lambda_{k}(t)\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(t)+\mu_{k}(t)\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(t)\big)\\
0&=\langle\boldsymbol{h}(t),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}(t)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{h}(t),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}(t)\rangle,\quad\,\forall k\in\{1,\dots,n\}.\end{split}

The pair of multipliers
(\vec{\lambda}(t),\vec{\mu}(t))
are introduced to ensure the orthogonality conditions in the second line above. In Section
10
we show (in Lemma
10.5
) that to each pair of
C^{1}
trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
as in (
1.46
) we can associate a unique solution
(\vec{\lambda}(t),\vec{\mu}(t),\boldsymbol{h}(t))
of (
1.47
) satisfying

(1.48)

\begin{split}\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}t\|\boldsymbol{h}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}<\delta{\ \ \text{and}\ \ }\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}t^{2-}\|\boldsymbol{h}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}<\infty,\end{split}

for some sufficiently small
\delta>0
, and

(1.49)

\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}t^{3-}\Big(\big|\lambda_{k}(t)+(-1)^{k}v_{k}(t)\big|\\
+\big|\mu_{k}(t)+(-1)^{k}2\kappa^{2}\big(e^{-(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t))}-e^{-(a_{k}(t)-a_{k-1}(t))}\big)\big|\Big)<\infty.

In light of (
1.39
) the goal is now to show that there is a unique pair of trajectories
(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
as in (
1.46
) that satisfy

(1.50)

\begin{split}a_{k}(t)=-(-1)^{k}\lambda_{k}(t),\quad v_{k}(t)=-(-1)^{k}\mu_{k}(t)\end{split}

for each
k=1,\dots,n
. In the terminology of Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, this means solving the
bifurcation equations
, using as input the estimates (
1.48
) and (
1.49
). This is accomplished in Section
11
(see Proposition
11.1
).

This analysis associates to each
\vec{a}_{0}
as in (
1.32
) a unique
n
-kink cluster with error
\boldsymbol{h}(t)
small in the sense of (
1.48
) and trajectories as in (
1.46
). Coupling this qualified uniqueness statement with the estimates in Theorem
1
we see that uniqueness extends to all kink
n
-clusters. This argument is done carefully in Section
12
along with the construction of the stable manifold
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and local stable manifold
\mathcal{M}_{n,\operatorname{loc}}
.

1.5.
Further discussion and related results

There are a few related contexts in which to consider the results in Section
1.3
. First, from the point of view of mathematical physics, they describe the particle-like nature of solitons – the dynamics of kink
n
-clusters are captured by the
2n
parameters giving the positions and momenta of the kinks and the evolutions of these parameters are in turn governed by an approximate
n
-body law of motion (the attractive Toda system). Second, in dispersive PDEs the problem of Soliton Resolution predicts that every finite energy solution to (
1.10
) eventually decouples into a multi-kink configuration and a radiative wave. Within this framework, one can distinguish two types of nonlinear interactions to be studied separately: soliton-radiation interaction and soliton-soliton interaction. We focus on the latter, restricting to the strongly-interacting regime. Lastly, viewing the results under the lens of dynamical systems, this work develops an invariant manifold theory of multi-solitons.

The particle-like character of solitons is a well-known phenomenon, see
[
41
, Chapter 1]

for a historical account.
The question of justification that the positions of solitons satisfy
an approximate
n
-body law of motion was considered for instance in
[
63
,
18
,
12
,
58
]
.

In their work on blow-up for nonlinear waves, Merle and Zaag
[
49
]

obtained a system of ODEs with exponential terms like in (
1.42
),
but which was a
gradient flow
and not an
n
-body problem.
The dynamical behavior of solutions of this system was described by Côte and Zaag
[
10
]
.

In relation with our proof of Theorem
2
,
we note that Brouwer’s theorem was previously used in constructions of multi-solitons,
but for a rather different purpose, namely in order to avoid the growth of linear unstable modes, see
[
8
,
9
]
.

Let us stress again that the main object of our study are
solutions approaching multi-soliton configurations in the strong energy norm, in other words we address the question of interaction of solitons
in the absence of radiation
. The first construction of a two-soliton solution with trajectories having asymptotically vanishing velocities was obtained by Krieger, Martel and Raphaël
[
36
]
,
see also
[
47
,
57
,
25
,
22
,
23
,
29
]
for other constructions
and
[
65
]
for related computations in the completely integrable setting.
Allowing for a radiation term seems to be currently out of reach for the classical scalar fields considered here,
the question of the asymptotic stability of the kink being
still unresolved, see for example
[
11
,
35
,
16
,
19
,
5
,
39
]
for recent results
on this and related problems.

A detailed understanding of soliton interactions, particularly the description of an “ejection” mechanism for solutions that leave a neighborhood of multi-solitons was used in our work on the Soliton Resolution problem for energy critical waves
[
27
,
32
,
28
,
30
]
.
Determining universal profiles of soliton collapse is crucial to
several earlier works on dispersive equations, which have been important inspirations.
We mention the study of center-stable manifolds of ground states for various nonlinear wave equations, see for instance the works of Nakanishi and Schlag
[
55
,
56
]
, and Krieger, Nakanishi, and Schlag
[
37
]
,
as well as the earlier work by Duyckaerts and Merle
[
14
]
. We also mention the classification results for solutions near the ground state soliton for the gKdV equation by Martel, Merle, and Raphaël
[
44
,
45
,
46
]
, particularly their description of the “exit” regime for solutions that leave a neighborhood of the soliton by remaining close to the minimal mass blow-up solution. A distinction between the present work and these earlier landmarks is that here we deal with multi-solitons.

Finally, we emphasize that our definition of kink clusters
concerns
only one time direction
, and our study
does not address the question of the behaviour of kink clusters as
t\to{-}\infty
, which goes by the name of the
kink collision problem
.
We refer to

[
34
]

for an overview,
and to
[
54
,
52
]
for recent rigorous results
in the case of the
\phi^{6}
model.

1.6.
Notation

Even if
v(x)
is a function of one variable
x
, we often write
\partial_{x}v(x)

instead of
v^{\prime}(x)
to denote the derivative. The prime notation is only used
for the time derivative of a function of one variable
t

and for the derivative of the potential
U
.

If
\vec{a},\vec{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\vec{a}\cdot\vec{b}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}b_{k}
.
If
u
and
v
are (real-valued) functions, then
\langle u,v\rangle\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u(x)v(x)\mathrm{\,d}x
.

Boldface is used for pairs of values
(which will usually be a pair of functions
forming an element of the phase space).
A small arrow above a letter indicates a vector
with any finite number of components
(which will usually be the number of kinks
or the number of kinks diminished by 1).
If
\boldsymbol{u}=(u,\dot{u})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\boldsymbol{v}=(v,\dot{v})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we write

\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\coloneqq\langle u,v\rangle+\langle\dot{u},\dot{v}\rangle
.

We will have to manipulate finite sequences and sums. In order to make the formulas reasonably compact,
we need to introduce appropriate notation, some of which is not completely standard.
If
\vec{w}=(w_{j})_{j}
is a vector and
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a function, we denote
f(\vec{w})
the vector with components
f(w_{j})
,
for example
e^{\vec{w}}
will denote the vector
(e^{w_{j}})_{j}
,
\vec{w}\,^{2}
the vector
(w_{j}^{2})_{j}
and
\log\vec{w}
the vector
(\log w_{j})_{j}
.
If
\vec{v}=(v_{j})_{j}
is another vector, we denote
\vec{w}\vec{v}\coloneqq(w_{j}v_{j})_{j}
.
We also denote
w_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{j}w_{j}
.

When we write
\simeq
,
\lesssim
or
\gtrsim
, it should be understood that the constant is allowed to depend only on
n
.
Our use of the symbol
f^{\sharp}\sim f
is non-standard and indicates that
f^{\sharp}-f
is a negligible
quantity (the meaning of “negligible” will be specified in each case),
without requiring that
f^{\sharp}/f
be close to
1
.

The energy space is denoted
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We also use local energies and energy norms defined as follows.
For
-\infty\leq x_{0}<x_{0}^{\prime}\leq\infty
and
\phi_{0}:[x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote

(1.51)

\displaystyle E_{p}(\phi_{0};x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+U(\phi_{0})\Big)\mathrm{\,d}x,

(1.52)

\displaystyle E(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0};x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\dot{\phi}_{0}(x))^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+U(\phi_{0})\Big)\mathrm{\,d}x,

(1.53)

\displaystyle\|\phi_{0}\|_{H^{1}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\big((\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+\phi_{0}(x)^{2}\big)\mathrm{\,d}x,

(1.54)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\big((\dot{\phi}_{0}(x))^{2}+(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x))^{2}+\phi_{0}(x)^{2}\big)\mathrm{\,d}x.

The open ball of center
c
and radius
r
in a normed space
A
is denoted
B_{A}(c,r)
.
We denote
\mathrm{D}
and
\mathrm{D}^{2}
the first and second Fréchet derivatives of a functional.

We write
x_{+}\coloneqq\max(0,x)
.

We take
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
to be a decreasing
C^{\infty}
function
such that
\chi(x)=1
for
x\leq\frac{1}{3}

and
\chi(x)=0
for
x\geq\frac{2}{3}
.

Proofs end with the sign
\boxvoid
. Statements given without proof end with the sign
\boxslash
.

2.
Kinks and interactions between them

2.1.
Stationary solutions

A stationary field
\phi(t,x)=\psi(x)
is a solution of (
1.10
) if and only if

(2.1)

\partial_{x}^{2}\psi(x)=U^{\prime}(\psi(x)),\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

We seek solutions of (
2.1
) having finite potential energy
E_{p}(\psi)
.
Since
U(\psi)\geq 0
for
\psi\in\mathbb{R}
, the condition
E_{p}(\psi)<\infty
implies

(2.2)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\psi(x))^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x<\infty,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}U(\psi(x))\mathrm{\,d}x<\infty.

From (
2.2
) we have
\psi\in C(\mathbb{R})
,
so (
2.1
) and
U\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
yield
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Multiplying (
2.1
) by
\partial_{x}\psi
we get

(2.4)

\partial_{x}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\psi)^{2}-U(\psi)\Big)=\partial_{x}\psi\big(\partial_{x}^{2}\psi-U^{\prime}(\psi)\big)=0,

so
\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\psi(x))^{2}-U(\psi(x))=k
is a constant.
But then (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) imply
k=0
.
We obtain first-order autonomous equations, called the Bogomolny equations,

(2.5)

\partial_{x}\psi(x)=\sqrt{2U(\psi(x))}\quad\text{or}\quad\partial_{x}\psi(x)=-\sqrt{2U(\psi(x))},\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

which can be integrated in the standard way, see for instance
[
26
, Section 2]
.
We conclude that:

•

the only stationary solution of (
1.10
) belonging to
\mathcal{E}_{1,1}
is the vacuum
state
\boldsymbol{1}\coloneqq(1,0)
and the only stationary solution of (
1.10
) belonging to
\mathcal{E}_{-1,-1}
is the vacuum
state
-\boldsymbol{1}\coloneqq(-1,0)
,

•

the only stationary solutions of (
1.10
) belonging to
\mathcal{E}_{-1,1}

are the translates of
(H,0)
, where the function
H
is defined by

(2.6)

H(x)=G^{-1}(x),\qquad\text{with}\ \ G(\psi)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\psi}\frac{\mathrm{\,d}y}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}\ \ \text{for all }\psi\in({-}1,1),

and the solutions of (
2.1
) belonging to
\mathcal{E}_{1,-1}

are the translates of
(-H,0)
.

The asymptotic behaviour of
H(x)
for
|x|
large is essential for our analysis.
We have the following result, which is a refinement of
[
26
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The function
H(x)
defined by (
2.6
) is odd, of class
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
and there exist constants
\kappa>0
and
C>0
such that for all
x\geq 0

(2.7)

\displaystyle\big|H(x)-1+\kappa e^{-x}-\frac{1}{6}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{-2x}\big|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}H(x)-\kappa e^{-x}+\frac{1}{3}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{-2x}\big|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}^{2}H(x)+\kappa e^{-x}-\frac{2}{3}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{-2x}\big|\leq Ce^{-3x},

and for all
x\leq 0

(2.8)

\begin{split}&\big|H(x)+1-\kappa e^{x}+\frac{1}{6}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{2x}\big|\\
+&\big|\partial_{x}H(x)-\kappa e^{x}+\frac{1}{3}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{2x}\big|\\
+&\big|\partial_{x}^{2}H(x)-\kappa e^{x}+\frac{2}{3}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{2x}\big|\leq Ce^{3x},\end{split}

Proof.

We only prove (
2.7
), which gives the asymptotic behavior as
x\to\infty
, since (
2.8
) then follows from the oddness of
H
. Recall from
[
26
, Proposition 2.1]
that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
so that for
\kappa>0
defined by

(2.9)

\begin{split}\kappa\coloneqq\exp\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}-\frac{1}{1-y}\big)\,\mathrm{\,d}y\Big)\end{split}

we have,

(2.10)

\begin{split}\big|H(x)-1+\kappa e^{-x}\big|\leq C_{1}e^{-2x}.\end{split}

Define
z
by the formula

(2.11)

\begin{split}\frac{1-H(x)}{\kappa}=e^{-x}(1-\frac{\kappa U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)}{6}e^{-z}).\end{split}

From the above we have

(2.12)

\begin{split}e^{-z}\leq C_{1}\frac{6}{\kappa^{2}}\left\lvert{U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)}\right\rvert^{-1}e^{-x}.\end{split}

Using the fourth order Taylor expansion of
U(y)
about
y=1
gives,

(2.13)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}-\frac{1}{1-y}-\frac{1}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)=O(1-y){\ \ \text{as}\ \ }y\nearrow 1\end{split}

and hence from (
2.6
) and (
1.30
),

(2.14)

\begin{split}G(\psi)&=-\log(1-\psi)+\frac{1}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)\int_{0}^{\psi}\mathrm{\,d}y+\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}-\frac{1}{1-y}-\frac{1}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)\Big)\,\mathrm{\,d}y\\
&\quad-\int_{\psi}^{1}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2U(y)}}-\frac{1}{1-y}-\frac{1}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)\Big)\,\mathrm{\,d}y\\
&=-\log\Big(\frac{1-\psi}{\kappa}\Big)-\frac{\kappa}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)\Big(\frac{1-\psi}{\kappa}\Big)+O((1-\psi)^{2}).\end{split}

Since
H(x)\coloneqq G^{-1}(x)
we arrive at

(2.15)

\begin{split}x=-\log\Big(\frac{1-H(x)}{\kappa}\Big)-\frac{\kappa}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)\Big(\frac{1-H(x)}{\kappa}\Big)+O((1-H(x))^{2}).\end{split}

Using (
2.11
) in the previous line yields,

(2.16)

\begin{split}\log\Big(1-\frac{\kappa U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)}{6}e^{-z}\Big)=\frac{\kappa U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)}{6}e^{-x}\Big(1-\frac{\kappa U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)}{6}e^{-z}\Big)+O(e^{-2x}).\end{split}

From (
2.12
) we then arrive at the estimate,

(2.17)

\begin{split}|e^{-z}-e^{-x}|\lesssim e^{-2x}\end{split}

provided
x>0
is sufficiently large.
Hence,

(2.18)

\begin{split}\big|H(x)+1-\kappa e^{x}+\frac{1}{6}\kappa^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)e^{2x}\big|\lesssim e^{-x}|e^{-z}-e^{-x}|\lesssim e^{-3x}\end{split}

as claimed. The bound for
\partial_{x}H(x)
follows from (
2.22
), the bound (
2.18
), and the expansion
\sqrt{2U(y)}=(1-y)-\frac{1}{6}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)(1-y)^{2}+O(|1-y|^{3})
. The bound for
\partial_{x}^{2}H
uses (
2.1
), (
2.18
), and the expansion
U^{\prime}(y)=-(1-y)+\frac{1}{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(1)(1-y)^{2}+O(|1-y|^{3})
.
∎

We denote

(2.19)

\displaystyle M\coloneqq\|\partial_{x}H\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=2\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{2U(y)}\mathrm{\,d}y=2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}U(H(x))\mathrm{\,d}x=E_{p}(H),

all these equalities following from (
2.5
) and the change of variable
y=H(x)
.
In the context of Special Relativity,
one can think of
M
as the (rest) mass of the kink.

We will also use the fact that

(2.20)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial_{x}H(x)\left(U^{\prime\prime}(H(x))-1\right)e^{x}\mathrm{\,d}x=-2\kappa,

and we observe the equipartition of the potential energy of the kink:

(2.21)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}H(x))^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x=\frac{1}{2}M=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}U(H(x))\mathrm{\,d}x.

These computations are checked in
[
26
, Section 2.1]
.

Finally, we recall the following
Bogomolny trick
from
[
3
]
.
If
\phi_{0}(x_{0})\leq\phi_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime})
(understood as limits if
x_{0}
or
x_{0}^{\prime}
is infinite), then

(2.22)

\displaystyle E_{p}(\phi_{0};x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\Big(\big(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}-\sqrt{2U(\phi_{0})}\big)^{2}+2\phi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{2U(\phi_{0})}\Big)\mathrm{\,d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\phi_{0}(x_{0})}^{\phi_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime})}\sqrt{2U(y)}\mathrm{\,d}y+\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}-\sqrt{2U(\phi_{0})}\big)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x.

Analogously, if
\phi_{0}(x_{0})\geq\phi_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime})
, then

(2.23)

E_{p}(\phi_{0};x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})=\int_{\phi_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime})}^{\phi_{0}(x_{0})}\sqrt{2U(y)}\mathrm{\,d}y+\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{x}\phi_{0}+\sqrt{2U(\phi_{0})}\big)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x.

Hence, restrictions of kinks and antikinks to (bounded or unbounded) intervals are minimisers of the potential
energy among all the functions connecting two given values in
(-1,1)
.

2.2.
Traveling kinks and their energies

The energy functional
E
is smooth on each energy sector. Its Fréchet derivatives are given by

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})(\boldsymbol{h})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big[\dot{\phi}(x)\dot{h}(x)+({-}\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(x)+U^{\prime}(\phi(x))h(x)\big]\mathrm{\,d}x,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})(\boldsymbol{h}_{1},\boldsymbol{h}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big[\dot{h}_{1}(x)\dot{h}_{2}(x)+\partial_{x}h_{1}(x)\partial_{x}h_{2}(x)+U^{\prime\prime}(\phi(x))h_{1}(x)h_{2}(x)\big]\mathrm{\,d}x,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{k}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})(\boldsymbol{h}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{h}_{k})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}U^{(k)}(\phi(x))\prod_{j=1}^{k}h_{j}(x)\mathrm{\,d}x,\qquad\text{for all }k\geq 3.

We write
\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\boldsymbol{h}_{1}\coloneqq(-\partial_{x}^{2}h_{1}+U^{\prime\prime}(\phi)h_{1},\dot{h}_{1})
,
so that
\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})(\boldsymbol{h}_{1},\boldsymbol{h}_{2})=\langle\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\boldsymbol{h}_{1},\boldsymbol{h}_{2}\rangle
.

We gather below the explicit expressions for
\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)
, its derivatives, along with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v),\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v)
defined in (
1.25
),
although we will hardly use them:

(2.27)

\begin{split}\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(0,v;x)&=\big({-}\gamma_{v}\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x),\gamma_{v}^{2}v\partial_{x}^{2}H(\gamma_{v}x)\big),\\
\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(0,v;x)&=\big(\gamma_{v}^{3}vx\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x),{-}\gamma_{v}^{3}\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x)-\gamma_{v}^{4}v^{2}x\partial_{x}^{2}H(\gamma_{v}x)\big),\\
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(0,v;x)&=\big(\gamma_{v}^{2}v\partial_{x}^{2}H(\gamma_{v}x),\gamma_{v}\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x)\big),\\
\boldsymbol{\beta}(0,v;x)&=\big({-}\gamma_{v}^{3}\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x)-\gamma_{v}^{4}v^{2}x\partial_{x}^{2}H(\gamma_{v}x),{-}\gamma_{v}^{3}vx\partial_{x}H(\gamma_{v}x)\big).\end{split}

We have set
a=0
, the general formulas being obtained
by translating the variable
x
.

We note the identities,

(2.28)

\begin{split}\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v)=-\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v),\quad\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v)=-\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v).\end{split}

Since
\boldsymbol{J}
is skew-symmetric, we have

(2.29)

\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v),\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v),\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle=0.

Differentiating (
1.19
) with respect to
v
, we obtain

(2.30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{v}^{3}M

\displaystyle=\partial_{v}P(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))={-}\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v),\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v),\boldsymbol{J}\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle

\displaystyle={-}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v),\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v),\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle=-\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v),\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)\rangle.

If we multiply both sides of (
1.16
) from the left by
{-}\boldsymbol{J}
, we get

(2.31)

\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))=v\boldsymbol{J}\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)=-v\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v),

and differentiation with respect to
a
yields

(2.32)

-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))(\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v))=\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)=v\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v).

If we differentiate (
2.31
) with respect to
v
, we get

(2.33)

-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))(\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v))=\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(a,v))\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a,v)=-\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a,v)+v\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\beta}(a,v).

2.3.
Interaction of the kinks

Our next goal is to compute the potential energy and the interaction forces
for a multikink configuration, which we recall is denoted by

(2.34)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v};x)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(\boldsymbol{H}_{k}(x)+\boldsymbol{1}),

where
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are the positions of the transitions,

\vec{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in(-1,1)^{n}
are the Lorentz parameters and
\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{H}(a_{k},v_{k})
.

We also recall that for
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

(2.35)

\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=(-1)^{k}\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\qquad\partial_{v_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=(-1)^{k}\partial_{v_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}.

We will tend to use the notation on the right hand side of these equalities in computations.

Recalling the definition of
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})
from (
1.21
), for
(\vec{a},\vec{v}):I\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\rho:I\to(0,\infty)
by

(2.36)

\rho(t)\coloneqq\rho(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{-(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t))}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{k}(t)^{2}.

In what follows we will always assume that
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\leq\eta_{0}
, where
\eta_{0}>0
is a small number to be eventually fixed. We also introduce the following shorthand notation.
Given a vector
\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dots\leq a_{n}
we denote

(2.37)

\begin{split}\vec{y}&=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\quad y_{k}\coloneqq a_{k+1}-a_{k}\\
y_{0}&\coloneqq+\infty,\quad y_{n}\coloneqq+\infty,\quad y_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}}y_{k}.\end{split}

Given
\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
v_{j}\in(-1,1)
for each
j
we set

(2.38)

\begin{split}v_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{k\in\{1,\dots,n\}}|v_{k}|\end{split}

and note that
\gamma_{v_{\min}}\geq 1
.

We next define the interaction force between the kinks for solutions near a multikink configuration. Using (
2.31
) we compute

(2.39)

\begin{split}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}_{k})+\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}_{k})\\
&=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}v_{k}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}+\begin{pmatrix}U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})\\
0\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

Letting
\eta_{0}<1
, let
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\vec{0},\sqrt{\eta_{0}}))
be given by

(2.40)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}v_{k}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})\\
0\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

The
k
th interaction force is the defined as

(2.41)

\begin{split}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})&\coloneqq(-1)^{k}\langle\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle=-(-1)^{k}\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle=-(-1)^{k}\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle\\
&=(-1)^{k}\big\langle\partial_{x}H(a_{k},v_{k}),\,U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})\big\rangle.\end{split}

Analogously as in (
1.45
), if the velocities are fixed to be zero, we set

(2.42)

F_{k}(\vec{a})\coloneqq F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{0}).

Observe that
F_{k}(\vec{a})=-\partial_{a_{k}}E_{\mathrm{p}}(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}))
, which is consistent with the analogy made by treating
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
as point masses. We also have
\sum_{k=1}^{n}F_{k}(\vec{a})=0
, due to the translation invariance of
E_{p}
, which we can view as a manifestation of Newton’s third law. Since we have introduced the (small) Lorentz parameter
\vec{v}
into the multikink ansatz, these identities no longer hold exactly, rather only approximately.
Indeed,
we observe the identity

(2.43)

\begin{split}\sum_{k=1}^{n}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(-1)^{j}\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,v_{j}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\rangle.\end{split}

To see this, note that by the translation invariance of the total energy

(2.44)

\begin{split}0=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial_{a_{k}}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\rangle.\end{split}

Rearranging the above and using (
2.40
) and (
2.41
) gives

(2.45)

\begin{split}\sum_{k=1}^{n}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(-1)^{j}\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,v_{j}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\rangle,\end{split}

as claimed. The following lemma quantifies the smallness of
\sum_{j=1}^{n}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There exist constants
\eta_{0}>0,C>0
so that for every
(\vec{a},\vec{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\leq\eta_{0}
,

(2.46)

\begin{split}\big|\sum_{k=1}^{n}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\big|\leq C|\vec{v}|y_{\min}e^{-\gamma_{v_{\min}}y_{\min}}.\end{split}

Lemma
2.2
is a straightforward consequence of the identity (
2.45
) and the following lemma, proved in
[
26
]
. We also use this next lemma in subsequent results estimating the size of interactions.

Lemma 2.3
.

[
26
, Proof of Lemma 2.5]

For any
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
\alpha,\beta>0
with
\alpha\neq\beta
the following bound holds:

(2.47)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\alpha(x-x_{1})_{+}}e^{-\beta(x_{2}-x)_{+}}\mathrm{\,d}x\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}e^{-\min(\alpha,\beta)(x_{2}-x_{1})}.

For any
\alpha>0
, the following bound holds:

(2.48)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\alpha(x-x_{1})_{+}}e^{-\alpha(x_{2}-x)_{+}}\mathrm{\,d}x\lesssim_{\alpha}(1+x_{2}-x_{1})e^{-\alpha(x_{2}-x_{1})}.

\boxslash

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

First observe that by (
2.29
) the terms with
j=k
on the right side of (
2.43
) vanish. Then, by Proposition (
2.1
) and Lemma
2.3
we have, for each
j\neq k
,

(2.49)

\begin{split}|\langle\partial_{a_{k}}\boldsymbol{H}_{k},\,v_{j}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\rangle|\lesssim|\vec{v}|^{2}y_{\min}e^{-\gamma_{v_{\min}}y_{\min}}\end{split}

which gives the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Denote by
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the function

(2.50)

f(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\coloneqq U^{\prime}\Big(1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(w_{k}+1)\Big)-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(w_{k}).

There exists
C
such that for all
\vec{w}\in[-2,2]^{n}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

(2.51)

\displaystyle\big|f(\vec{w})+(-1)^{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(w_{k})-1)((1+w_{k+1})-(1-w_{k-1}))\big|\leq

\displaystyle C\big(\max_{j<k-1}|1-w_{j}|+\max_{j>k+1}|1+w_{j}|+

\displaystyle+|1-w_{k}||1+w_{k+1}|^{2}+|1+w_{k}||1-w_{k-1}|^{2}+|1+w_{k+1}||1-w_{k-1}|\big),

where by convention
w_{0}\coloneqq 1
,
w_{n+1}\coloneqq-1
and
\max_{j<0}|1-w_{j}|=\max_{j>n+1}|1+w_{j}|=0
.

Proof.

Let
\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be given by
v_{j}\coloneqq 1
for
j<k
,
v_{j}\coloneqq-1
for
j>k
and
v_{k}\coloneqq w_{k}
.
The Taylor formula yields

(2.52)

\displaystyle f(\vec{w})

\displaystyle=f(\vec{v})+(\vec{w}-\vec{v})\cdot{\nabla}f(\vec{v})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(w_{i}-v_{i})(w_{j}-v_{j})\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f((1-t)\vec{v}+t\vec{w})\mathrm{\,d}t.

We compute and estimate all the terms, calling a quantity “negligible”
if its absolute value is smaller than the right hand side of (
2.51
).
For all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\vec{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

(2.53)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}f(\vec{u})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j}\Big(U^{\prime\prime}\Big(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}+1)\Big)-U^{\prime\prime}(u_{j})\Big),

(2.54)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f(\vec{u})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+j}U^{\prime\prime\prime}\Big(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}+1)\Big),\qquad\text{if }i\neq j,

(2.55)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}^{2}f(\vec{u})

\displaystyle=U^{\prime\prime\prime}\Big(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}+1)\Big)-(-1)^{j}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(u_{j}).

Observe that

(2.56)

1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(v_{\ell}+1)=1+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{\ell}+(-1)^{k}(w_{k}+1)=(-1)^{k}w_{k},

in particular, since
U^{\prime\prime}
is even and
U^{\prime\prime}(1)=1
, for all
j\neq k
we have
\partial_{j}f(\vec{v})=(-1)^{j}(U^{\prime\prime}(w_{k})-1)
.
We thus obtain

(2.57)

\displaystyle(\vec{w}-\vec{v})\cdot{\nabla}f(\vec{v})

\displaystyle\sim(w_{k-1}-1)\partial_{{k-1}}f(\vec{v})+(w_{k+1}+1)\partial_{{k+1}}f(\vec{v})

\displaystyle={-}(-1)^{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(w_{k})-1)((1+w_{k+1})-(1-w_{k-1})).

Remains the second line of (
2.52
). The terms with
i\notin\{k-1,k+1\}

or
j\notin\{k-1,k+1\}
are clearly negligible,
as are the terms
(i,j)\in\{(k-1,k+1),(k+1,k-1)\}
,
hence it suffices to consider the cases
(i,j)\in\{(k-1,k-1),(k+1,k+1)\}
.
Since the latter is analogous to the former,
we only consider
i=j=k-1
,
in particular we assume
k\geq 2
.

Using (
2.55
), the fact that
U^{\prime\prime\prime}
is locally Lipschitz, and writing

(2.58)

1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}+1)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-2}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}-1)+(-1)^{k-1}u_{k-1}+\sum_{\ell=k}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(u_{\ell}+1),

we obtain

(2.59)

|\partial_{k-1}^{2}f(\vec{u})|\lesssim\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-2}|1-u_{\ell}|+\sum_{\ell=k}^{n}|1+u_{\ell}|.

Setting
\vec{u}\coloneqq(1-t)\vec{v}+t\vec{w}

and multiplying by
(w_{k-1}-v_{k-1})^{2}=(1-w_{k-1})^{2}
,
we obtain a negligible term as claimed.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

There exists
C
such that for all
\vec{w}\in[-2,2]^{n}

(2.60)

\displaystyle\Big|U\Big(1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(w_{k}+1)\Big)-\sum_{k=1}^{n}U(w_{k})\Big|\leq C\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|1-w_{i}||1+w_{j}|,

(2.61)

\displaystyle\Big|U^{\prime}\Big(1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}(w_{k}+1)\Big)-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(w_{k})\Big|\leq C\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|1-w_{i}||1+w_{j}|.

Proof.

We proceed by induction with respect to
n
. For
n=1
, both sides of both estimates equal
0
.

Let
n>1
and set

(2.62)

v\coloneqq 1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}(w_{k}+1)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}(w_{k}-1)-(-1)^{n}.

Integrating the bound
|U^{\prime}(w+(-1)^{n}(w_{n}+1))-U^{\prime}(w)|\lesssim|w_{n}+1|

for
w
between
-(-1)^{n}
and
v
, and using
U({-}(-1)^{n})=0
as well as
U((-1)^{n}w_{n})=U(w_{n})
, we get

(2.63)

\displaystyle|U(v+(-1)^{n}(w_{n}+1))-U(w_{n})-U(v)|

\displaystyle\leq C|(-1)^{n}+v||1+w_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq C|1+w_{n}|\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}|1-w_{k}|,

which finishes the induction step for (
2.60
).

Integrating the bound
|U^{\prime\prime}(w+(-1)^{n}(w_{n}+1))-U^{\prime\prime}(w)|\lesssim|w_{n}+1|

for
w
between
-(-1)^{n}
and
v
, and using
U^{\prime}({-}(-1)^{n})=0
as well as
U^{\prime}((-1)^{n}w_{n})=(-1)^{n}U^{\prime}(w_{n})
, we get

(2.64)

\displaystyle|U^{\prime}(v+(-1)^{n}(w_{n}+1))-(-1)^{n}U^{\prime}(w_{n})-U^{\prime}(v)|

\displaystyle\leq C|(-1)^{n}+v||1+w_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq C|1+w_{n}|\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}|1-w_{k}|,

which finishes the induction step for (
2.61
).
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

There exists constants
C>0,\eta_{0}>0
such that for every
(\vec{a},\vec{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\leq\eta_{0}

(2.65)

\begin{split}\|U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}}\end{split}

and

(2.66)

\begin{split}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\|\partial_{a_{k}}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\|_{H^{1}}&\leq C\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}}\\
\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\|\partial_{v_{k}}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\|_{H^{1}}&\leq C\left\lvert{\vec{v}}\right\rvert\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}}.\end{split}

Remark 2.7
.

Using the identity (
2.39
) we see that that above implies the estimates

(2.67)

\begin{split}\|\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}v_{k}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\|_{H^{1}\times H^{2}}\leq C\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}},\\
\end{split}

or

(2.68)

\|\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}v_{k}\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}}.

Also, setting
\vec{v}=0
in Lemma
2.6
yields the estimates

(2.69)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}E_{\mathrm{p}}(H(\vec{a}))\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\sqrt{y_{\mathrm{min}}}e^{-y_{\mathrm{min}}},

(2.70)

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\big\|\partial_{x}H(\cdot-a_{k})\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\cdot-a_{k}))\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\sqrt{y_{\mathrm{min}}}e^{-y_{\mathrm{min}}}.

for every increasing
n
-tuple
\vec{a}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

We see that (
2.61
) yields

(2.71)

\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\Big|\lesssim\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|1-H_{i}||1+H_{j}|.

After taking the square, integrating over
\mathbb{R}
and applying Lemma
2.3
, we obtain the
L^{2}
bound in (
2.69
) since
\gamma_{v_{\min}}\geq 1
.

In order to prove the
L^{2}
bound in the first estimate in (
2.66
), we write

(2.72)

H(\vec{a},\vec{v})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{\ell}(H_{\ell}-1)+(-1)^{k}H_{k}+\sum_{\ell=k+1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}(H_{\ell}+1).

Since
U^{\prime\prime}
is locally Lipschitz and
|\partial_{x}H|\lesssim\min(|1-H|,|1+H|)
, we obtain

(2.73)

\big|\partial_{x}H_{k}\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\big|\lesssim\max_{1\leq\ell<k}|1-H_{\ell}||1+H_{k}|+\max_{k<\ell\leq n}|1-H_{k}||1+H_{\ell}|,

and we conclude as above.

To conclude the
H^{1}
component of these estimates we note the computations

(2.74)

\begin{split}\partial_{x}\big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H_{k})\big)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\end{split}

and

(2.75)

\begin{split}\partial_{x}\big(\partial_{a_{k}}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\big)&=\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\\
&\quad-(\partial_{x}H_{k})^{2}\Big((-1)^{k}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime\prime}(H_{k})\Big)\\
&\quad-\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}(\partial_{x}H_{k})(\partial_{x}H_{j})U^{\prime\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})).\end{split}

and proceed as above. The second estimate in (
2.66
) is similar.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

There exists
C,\eta_{0}>0
such that for every
(\vec{a},\vec{v})
such that
\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})\leq\eta_{0}

(2.76)

\displaystyle\big|F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-2\kappa^{2}(e^{-y_{k}}-e^{-y_{k-1}})\big|\leq Cy_{\min}e^{-2y_{\min}}+C|v|^{2}y_{\min}e^{-y_{\min}},

where
\kappa
is defined in Proposition
2.1
.

Proof.

We first show that

(2.77)

\begin{split}\big|F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{0})\big|\lesssim|v|^{2}y_{\min}e^{-y_{\min}}.\end{split}

To see this, we write

(2.78)

\begin{split}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{0})&=(-1)^{k}\langle\partial_{a}H(a_{k},0)-\partial_{a}H_{k},\,f(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle\\
&\quad+(-1)^{k}\langle H(a_{k},0),\,f(\vec{a},\vec{0})-f(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle\end{split}

where we are writing
\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})=(f(\vec{a},\vec{v}),0)
; see (
2.40
).
For the first term on the right we use the estimate

(2.79)

\begin{split}\|\partial_{a}H(a_{k},0)-\partial_{a}H(a_{k},v_{k})\|_{L^{2}}&=\|\int_{0}^{v_{k}}\partial_{v}\partial_{a}H(a_{k},v)\,\mathrm{\,d}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|\vec{v}|^{2}\Big(\|x\partial_{x}^{2}H\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{x}H\|_{L^{2}}\Big)\end{split}

which, using (
2.65
) and the definition of
\boldsymbol{f}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
, gives

(2.80)

\begin{split}|\langle\partial_{a}H(a_{k},0)-\partial_{a}H_{k},\,f(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle|\lesssim|\vec{v}|^{2}\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}}.\end{split}

For the second term, given a vector
\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we introduce the temporary notation
\vec{v}(j)=(0,\dots,0,v_{j+1},\dots v_{n})
with the conventions
\vec{v}(0)=\vec{v}
and
\vec{v}(n)=\vec{0}
. and write

(2.81)

\begin{split}f(\vec{a},\vec{0})-f(\vec{a},\vec{v})=\sum_{j=0}^{n}f(\vec{a},\vec{v}(j))-f(\vec{a},\vec{v}(j+1)).\end{split}

For each of the terms on the right we have

(2.82)

\begin{split}&\|f(\vec{a},\vec{v}(j))-f(\vec{a},\vec{v}(j+1))\|_{L^{2}}=\Big\|\int_{0}^{v_{j+1}}\partial_{v_{j+1}}f(\vec{a},(0,\dots,v,v_{j+2},\dots,v_{n}))\,\mathrm{\,d}v\Big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{v_{j+1}}\Big\|\partial_{v}H(a_{j+1},v)\Big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},(0,\dots,v,v_{j+2},\dots,v_{n}))-U^{\prime\prime}(a_{j+1},v)\Big)\Big\|_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{\,d}v.\end{split}

Using (
2.66
) and the above we conclude that

(2.83)

\begin{split}|\langle H(a_{k},0),\,f(\vec{a},\vec{0})-f(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle|\lesssim|v|^{2}\sqrt{y_{\min}}e^{-y_{\min}},\end{split}

giving (
2.77
). We have thus reduced to the case
\vec{v}=\vec{0}
, which we will assume for the remainder of the computations in the proof.
By Proposition
2.1
and Lemma
2.3
, we have

(2.84)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}H_{k}|\big(\max_{j<k-1}|1-H_{j}|+\max_{j>k+1}|1+H_{j}|+|1-H_{k}||1+H_{k+1}|^{2}+

\displaystyle+|1+H_{k}||1-H_{k-1}|^{2}+|1+H_{k+1}||1-H_{k-1}|\big)\mathrm{\,d}x\lesssim y_{\min}e^{-2y_{\min}}.

Thus, (
2.41
) and (
2.51
) yield

(2.85)

\Big|F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{0})+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial_{x}H_{k}(U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})-1)((1+H_{k+1})-(1-H_{k-1}))\mathrm{\,d}x\Big|\lesssim y_{\min}e^{-2y_{\min}}.
