\begin{split}&(a_{k}^{\prime}-v_{k})\Big(((-1)^{k}\gamma_{v_{k}}^{3}M-\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle\Big)-v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle+\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}\big(a_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle+v_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle\big)\\
&=-\sum_{j\neq k}v_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\rangle+\langle U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j}),\,\partial_{v}H_{k}\rangle+\langle g,\,\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\partial_{v}H_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\big(\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})-\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g}\big),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle.\end{split}

and

(4.38)

\begin{split}&(a_{k}^{\prime}-v_{k})\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-v_{k}^{\prime}\Big((-1)^{k}\gamma_{v_{k}}^{3}M-\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\Big)+\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}\big(a_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+v_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\big)\\
&=-(-1)^{k}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\langle\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\partial_{a}H_{k},\,g\rangle-\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\big(\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})-\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g}\big),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle.\end{split}

In order to justify (
4.37
) and (
4.38
), first assume that
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in C^{1}(I;\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}})

and (
1.10
) holds in the strong sense. Since the map
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}\mapsto\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0})

is of class
C^{1}
, we obtain
\vec{a}\in C^{1}(I;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\vec{v}\in C^{1}(I;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We pair (
4.2
) with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}(t)
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}(t)
to obtain

(4.39)

\begin{split}\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}\big(a_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+v_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\big)\\
\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}\big(a_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle+v_{j}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle\big)\end{split}

We expand the first term on the right of the first equation as

(4.40)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})&+\boldsymbol{g}),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\big(\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})-\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g}\big),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\end{split}

For the first term on the right we use (
2.32
) to write

(4.41)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\big(\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}_{k}))\big)\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\rangle+v_{k}\langle\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{g}\rangle\\
&=\langle\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\partial_{a}H_{k},\,g\rangle+v_{k}\langle\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{g}\rangle\end{split}

For the second term we have

(4.42)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})),\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle&=-\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+\langle U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j}),\,\partial_{a}H_{k}\rangle\\
&=-\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-(-1)^{k}F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\end{split}

For the left-hand side of (
4.39
) we use the relations

(4.43)

\begin{split}-a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\\
-a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle-v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle,\end{split}

which come from differentiating in the time the orthogonality conditions (
4.3
). After rearranging, we obtain (
4.38
).

Next, we expand

(4.44)

\begin{split}\langle&\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\boldsymbol{J}\big(\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})-\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g}\big),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle\end{split}

We apply (
2.33
) for the first term which, using (
4.3
) becomes

(4.45)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}))\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}_{k})\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle+\langle g,\,\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\partial_{v}H_{k}\rangle\\
&=-v_{k}\langle\partial_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k},\,\boldsymbol{g}\rangle+\langle g,\,\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\big)\partial_{v}H_{k}\rangle\end{split}

For the second term we have

(4.46)

\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{J}\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})),\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}\rangle&=(-1)^{k}\gamma_{v_{k}}^{3}Mv_{k}-\sum_{j\neq k}v_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j},\,\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j}),\,\partial_{v}H_{k}\rangle\end{split}

Using again (
4.43
) for the left hand-side of (
4.39
) and rearranging we obtain (
4.37
).

Consider now the general case (without the additional regularity assumptions).
By Proposition
3.2
, there exists a sequence
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
satisfying
(
4.34
) and the additional regularity assumption stated above,
hence the corresponding parameters
(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})
satisfy (
4.36
).
They also satisfy (
4.37
) and (
4.38
) with
(\vec{a},\vec{v})
replaced by
(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})
,
which is a linear system for
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{a}_{m}(t)
and
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{v}_{m}(t)
.
The non-diagonal terms of its matrix are

\ll\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})}
; see (
2.136
).
It follows from (
4.32
) that the diagonal terms differ from
(-1)^{k}M
by a quantity of order at most
\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})}
; see (
2.135
) and note that
\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\lesssim\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})}
.
In particular, the matrix is uniformly non-degenerate, hence we have uniform convergence
of
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{a}_{m}
and
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{v}_{m}
, which implies that
\vec{a}\in C^{1}
,
\vec{v}\in C^{1}
and (
4.37
) holds.

Step 2.
(Coercivity.)
If we choose
C_{1}\coloneqq 2C_{0}
, where
C_{1}
is the constant in (
4.31
)
and
C_{0}
the constant in (
4.8
), then we have

(4.47)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{g}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\rho(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\frac{1}{2}C_{1}\big(E(\boldsymbol{\phi})-nM\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\rho(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\frac{1}{2}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\rho(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\frac{1}{2}\big(\rho(\vec{a}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{g}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\big),

the last inequality resulting from the definition of
{\bf d}
.
This proves the first inequality in (
4.32
), up to adjusting
C_{0}
.
The second inequality in (
4.32
) follows from (
4.7
).

By standard estimates on the inverse of a diagonally dominant matrix, (
4.37
), (
4.38
)
and (
4.32
) yield (
4.33
).

Step 3.
(Continuous dependence of first derivatives.)
We have already observed that (
4.34
) implies (
4.36
).
It is clear that (
4.34
) implies uniform convergence of the coefficients
of the system (
4.37
) and (
4.38
), hence uniform convergence of
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{a}_{m}
and
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{v}_{m}
,
and we conlude that (
4.34
) implies (
4.35
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

We refer to

[
18
, Proposition 3]
,
[
50
, Proposition 3.1]
or
[
24
, Lemma 3.3]
for results and proofs similar to Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2
.

Remark 4.5
.

In the setting of Lemma
4.2
,
assume in addition that
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})=nM
. Then, a direct consequence of the estimate (
4.8
) is the bound

(4.48)

\|\dot{g}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\|g(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+M|\vec{v}(t)|^{2}\leq 4\kappa^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{-(a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t))}+C_{0}\rho(t)^{\frac{3}{2}},

where
\nu>0
is the constant in Lemma
2.13
.
This more precise coercivity bound will be useful in Section
6
.

4.2.
Refined modulation

In order to proceed with the analysis of the dynamics of the modulations parameters,
we follow an idea used in a similar context in
[
23
]
and introduce
localized momenta
, see also
[
59
, Proposition 4.3]
.

Recall that
\chi\in C^{\infty}
is a decreasing function such that
\chi(x)=1

for all
x\leq\frac{1}{3}
and
\chi(x)=0
for all
x\geq\frac{2}{3}
.

Definition 4.6

(Localized momenta)
.

Let
I
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}
,
\vec{a}
,
\vec{v}
and
\boldsymbol{g}
be as in Lemma
4.2
.
We set

(4.49)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\chi\Big(\frac{x-a_{1}(t)}{a_{2}(t)-a_{1}(t)}\Big),

\displaystyle\chi_{k}(t,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\chi\Big(\frac{x-a_{k}(t)}{a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t)}\Big)-\chi\Big(\frac{x-a_{k-1}(t)}{a_{k}(t)-a_{k-1}(t)}\Big),\qquad\text{for }k\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\},

\displaystyle\chi_{n}(t,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\chi\Big(\frac{x-a_{n-1}(t)}{a_{n}(t)-a_{n-1}(t)}\Big).

We define
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}):I\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

(4.50)

p_{k}(t)\coloneqq-\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)+\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}g(t),\dot{\phi}(t)\rangle.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exists
C_{0}
such that, under the assumptions of Lemma
4.2
,

\vec{p}\in C^{1}(I;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the following bounds hold for all
t\in I
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
:

(4.51)

\displaystyle|Mv_{k}(t)-p_{k}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\rho(t),

(4.52)

\displaystyle|p_{k}^{\prime}(t)-F_{k}(\vec{a}(t))|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{0}\rho(t)}{-\log\rho(t)},

where
M
and
F_{k}
are defined by (
2.19
) and (
2.42
).

Remark 4.8
.

If we think of
M
,
p_{k}
and
F_{k}
as the (rest) mass of the kink,
its momentum and the force acting on it, then (
4.51
) and (
4.52
)
yield (approximate) Newton’s second law for the kink motion.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

We start with the bound (
4.51
). To see this, we pair
\dot{\phi}(t)
with
\dot{\alpha}_{k}(t)
giving

(4.53)

\begin{split}\langle\dot{\phi},\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle=\langle\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+\langle\dot{g},\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle&=\langle\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-\langle g,\,\alpha_{k}\rangle,\end{split}

due to the orthogonality
\langle\boldsymbol{g},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\rangle=0
. We expand the right-hand side

(4.54)

\begin{split}\langle\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-\langle g,\,\alpha_{k}\rangle&=(-1)^{k}\langle\dot{H}_{k},\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle+O(|\vec{v}|y_{\min}e^{-y_{\min}}+|v|\|\boldsymbol{g}\|_{\mathcal{E}})\\
&=-(-1)^{k}v_{k}\gamma_{v_{k}}M+O(|\vec{v}|y_{\min}e^{-y_{\min}}+|v|\|\boldsymbol{g}\|_{\mathcal{E}}),\end{split}

where the first line uses Lemma
2.3
and the second the definition of
\dot{H}_{k}
and
\dot{\alpha}_{k}
. Using
\gamma_{v_{k}}=1+O(|\vec{v}|^{2})
, (
4.32
), and

(4.55)

\begin{split}|\langle\dot{\phi},\,\dot{\alpha}_{k}\rangle-\langle\dot{\phi},\,\dot{\alpha}(a_{k},0)\rangle|\lesssim\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v}),\end{split}

we arrive at the expression

(4.56)

\begin{split}Mv_{k}=-(-1)^{k}\langle\dot{\phi},\,\partial_{x}H(\cdot-a)\rangle+O(\rho(\vec{a},\vec{v})),\end{split}

and (
4.51
) follows from the above, (
4.50
), and (
4.32
).

It is clear that
\vec{p}\in C(I;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We claim that
\vec{p}\in C^{1}(I;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and for any

k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

(4.57)

\begin{split}&p_{k}^{\prime}=(-1)^{k}a_{k}^{\prime}\langle(1-\chi_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{\phi}\rangle+(-1)^{k}v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\partial_{x}H_{k},\dot{g}\rangle\\
&\quad+\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}\Big\langle\Big((a_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j})\partial_{x}\partial_{a}H_{j}+v_{j}^{\prime}\partial_{x}\partial_{v}H_{j}\Big),\,\dot{\phi}\Big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}v_{k}^{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}H_{k}\rangle\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{g}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi\partial_{x}g,\partial_{x}g\rangle+\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle-\langle\partial_{t}\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\dot{\phi}\rangle\\
&\quad+(-1)^{k}v_{k}\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k}\rangle+\langle\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\partial_{x}(\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k}\rangle)\\
&\quad-(-1)^{k}(1-\gamma_{v_{k}}^{2})\big\langle U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\partial_{x}H_{k},\,g\big\rangle-(-1)^{k}\big\langle(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\partial_{x}H_{k},\,g\big\rangle\\
&\quad+\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}\Big\rangle\\
&\quad+\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})\Big)\Big\rangle\\
&\quad+\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

In order to justify (
4.57
), first assume that

\partial_{x}g,\dot{g}\in C(I;H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))\cap C^{1}(I;L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
that first component of (
4.2
), which is

(4.58)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}g=\dot{g}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}\Big((a_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j})\partial_{a}H_{j}+v_{j}^{\prime}\partial_{v}H_{j}\Big)\end{split}

holds in the strong sense in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
and that the second component of (
4.2
), which can be expanded as

(4.59)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\dot{g}&=\partial_{x}^{2}g-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g-\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big)\\
&\quad-\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})\Big)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}(a_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j})\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}+v_{j}^{\prime}\partial_{v}\dot{H}_{j})\end{split}

holds in the strong sense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Using the above we also record the expression

(4.60)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\dot{\phi}&=\partial_{x}^{2}g-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g-\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big)\\
&\quad-\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})\Big)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}.\end{split}

From (
4.50
) and the Leibniz rule, we obtain

(4.61)

\begin{split}p_{k}^{\prime}&=(-1)^{k}a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\dot{\phi}\rangle+(-1)^{k}v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\partial_{x}H_{k},\dot{g}\rangle-\langle\partial_{t}\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\dot{\phi}\rangle\\
&-\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}\Big(\dot{g}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}\Big((a_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j})\partial_{a}H_{j}+v_{j}^{\prime}\partial_{v}H_{j}\Big),\,\dot{\phi}\Big\rangle\\
&-\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}^{2}g-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big\rangle-\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\partial_{x}^{2}g\Big\rangle\\
&+\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big)\Big\rangle\\
&+\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})+g)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}U^{\prime}(H_{j})\Big)\Big\rangle\\
&+\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{j}v_{j}\partial_{a}\dot{H}_{j}\Big\rangle.\end{split}

In the second line we expand a few of the terms as follows. First, we integrate by parts to obtain

(4.62)

\begin{split}-\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}\dot{g},\,\dot{\phi}\rangle&=-(-1)^{k}v_{k}\langle\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k}\rangle+\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}\dot{g},\,\partial_{x}(\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{g}\rangle\\
&\quad+\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle.\end{split}

Then note that

(4.63)

\begin{split}(-1)^{k}(a_{k}^{\prime}-v_{k})\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}\partial_{a}H_{k},\,\dot{\phi}\rangle&=-(-1)^{k}a_{k}^{\prime}\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{\phi}\rangle+(-1)^{k}v_{k}\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{g}\rangle\\
&+\frac{1}{2}v_{k}^{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}H_{k}\rangle+(-1)^{k}v_{k}\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k}\rangle,\end{split}

where in the last line we used that
\langle\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}H_{k}\rangle=0
.
Thus the first line added to the second line reduces to

(4.64)

(-1)^{k}a_{k}^{\prime}\langle(1-\chi_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{\phi}\rangle+(-1)^{k}v_{k}^{\prime}\langle\partial_{v}\partial_{x}H_{k},\dot{g}\rangle-\langle\partial_{t}\chi_{k}\partial_{x}g,\,\dot{\phi}\rangle+\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})\rangle\\
+\langle\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\partial_{x}(\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\dot{g},\,\dot{g}\rangle+v_{k}^{2}\langle(1-\chi_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}H_{k}\rangle\\
+(-1)^{k}v_{k}\langle\chi_{k}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k},\,\dot{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})-\dot{H}_{k}\rangle+\sum_{j\neq k}(-1)^{j}\Big((a_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j})\partial_{x}\partial_{a}H_{j}+v_{j}^{\prime}\partial_{x}\partial_{v}H_{j}\Big),\,\dot{\phi}\Big\rangle.

Next, consider the third line of (
4.61
). We use (
2.114
) to obtain

(4.65)

\begin{split}-\Big\langle(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k},\,\partial_{x}^{2}g-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v}))g\Big\rangle&=-(-1)^{k}(1-\gamma_{v_{k}}^{2})\big\langle U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k})\partial_{x}H_{k},\,g\big\rangle\\
&\quad-(-1)^{k}\big\langle(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a},\vec{v})-U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}))\partial_{x}H_{k},\,g\big\rangle\end{split}

and

(4.66)

\displaystyle\langle\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}g(t),\partial_{x}^{2}g(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\chi_{k}(t)(\partial_{x}g(t))^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x.

Inserting these relations into (
4.61
) and rearranging
the terms, we obtain (
4.57
).

Consider now the general case (without the additional regularity assumptions). By Proposition
3.2
,
there exists a sequence of solutions
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
satisfying (
4.34
) and the additional regularity assumptions stated above.
Let
\vec{p}_{m}
be the corresponding localized momentum.
By (
4.35
) and (
4.57
),
the sequence
\big(\vec{p}_{m}\big)_{m}
converges in
C^{1}
.

It remains to prove (
4.52
). In the computation below, we call a term “negligible”
if its absolute value is smaller than the right hand side of (
4.52
).

By Proposition
2.1
and the definition of
\chi_{k}
, we have

(4.67)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big((1-\chi_{k}(t))\partial_{x}^{2}H_{k}(t)\big)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\frac{1}{3}y_{\min}(t)}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}H)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x\lesssim e^{-\frac{2}{3}y_{\min}(t)}\lesssim\rho(t)^{\frac{2}{3}}.

By a similar computation, for all
j\neq k
we have

(4.68)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}^{2}H_{j}(t)\big)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x\lesssim\rho(t)^{\frac{2}{3}}.

Applying (
4.32
), (
4.33
), the bound
\|\dot{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\rho(t)}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
we obtain that the first line and first term of the second line of (
4.57
) are at most of order
\rho(t)^{\frac{4}{3}}
,
hence negligible. Next, we observe that
\|\partial_{x}\chi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim y_{\min}^{-1}\lesssim({-}\log\rho(t))^{-1}
. And, the Chain Rule and (
4.33
) yield
\|\partial_{t}\chi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim({-}\log\rho(t))^{-1}\sqrt{\rho(t)}
, hence the last term of the second line along with the third line of (
4.57
) is
negligible as well. The fourth line is seen to be negligible after an application of Cauchy-Schwarz and Lemma
2.3
. Similarly, the fifth line is negligible.

By Lemma
2.7
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the fourth line of (
4.57
)
differs by a term of order
\sqrt{y_{\min}(t)}e^{-\frac{3}{2}y_{\min}(t)}\ll\rho(t)({-}\log\rho(t))^{-1}
from

(4.69)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}g(t)\big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+g(t))-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))\big)\mathrm{\,d}x.

We now transform the last integral of the right hand side of (
4.57
). By (
2.70
), we have

(4.70)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)U^{\prime\prime}(H_{k}(t))g(t)\mathrm{\,d}x\sim\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))g(t)\mathrm{\,d}x.

Recalling the definition of
F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
, see (
2.41
), we thus obtain
that the last integral of the right hand side of (
4.57
) differs by a negligible term from

(4.71)

\displaystyle F_{k}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+(-1)^{k}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)\big(

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+g(t))

\displaystyle-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))g(t)\big)\mathrm{\,d}x.

Applying the Taylor formula pointwise and using the fact that

\|\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))-(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{3}y_{\min}(t)},

we see that up to negligible terms, in (
4.71
) we can replace

(-1)^{k}\partial_{x}H_{k}(t)
by
\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))
.
Recalling (
4.69
), we thus obtain

(4.72)

\displaystyle p_{k}^{\prime}(t)\sim F_{k}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))

\displaystyle+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{k}(t)\partial_{x}\big(U(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+g(t))-U(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)))g(t)\big)\mathrm{\,d}x,

and an integration by parts shows that the second line is negligible. Finally, we recall from (
2.42
) and (
2.77
) that
F_{k}(\vec{a},\vec{v})
and
F_{k}(\vec{a})
differ by a negligible term.
∎

5.
Characterizations of kink clusters

5.1.
Kink clusters approach multikink configurations

We now give a proof of Proposition
1.3
.
The essential ingredient is the following lemma
yielding the strong convergence of minimising sequences,
similar to the results in
[
53
, Appendix A]
.

Lemma 5.1
.

(1)

If the sequences
x_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{m}:({-}\infty,x_{m}]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy

(5.1)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{m}(x)=-1\quad\text{for all }m,\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi_{m};-\infty,x_{m})=0,\end{gathered}

then
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\phi_{m}+1\|_{H^{1}({-}\infty,x_{m})}=0
.

(2)

If the sequences
x_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{m}:[x_{m}^{\prime},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy

(5.2)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{m}(x)=1\quad\text{for all }m,\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi_{m};x_{m}^{\prime},\infty)=0,\end{gathered}

then
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\phi_{m}-1\|_{H^{1}(x_{m}^{\prime},\infty)}=0
.

(3)

If the sequence
\phi_{m}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(5.3)

\begin{gathered}\phi_{m}(0)=0,\quad\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi_{m}(x)=-1,\quad\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi_{m}(x)=1\quad\text{for all }m,\\
\lim_{m\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi_{m})=M,\end{gathered}

then
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\phi_{m}-H\|_{H^{1}}=0
.

(4)

If the sequences
x_{m}<0
,
x_{m}^{\prime}>0
and
\phi_{m}:[x_{m},x_{m}^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy

(5.4)

\begin{gathered}\phi_{m}(0)=0,\quad\lim_{m\to-\infty}\phi_{m}(x_{m})=-1,\quad\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi_{m}(x_{m}^{\prime})=1\quad\text{for all }m,\\
\lim_{m\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi_{m};x_{m},x_{m}^{\prime})=M,\end{gathered}

then
\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}=-\infty
,
\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}^{\prime}=\infty
and

\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\phi_{m}-H\|_{H^{1}(x_{m},x_{m}^{\prime})}=0
.

Proof.

It follows from (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) that

(5.5)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{x\leq x_{m}}\int_{-1}^{\phi_{m}(x)}\sqrt{2U(y)}\mathrm{\,d}y=0,

hence

(5.6)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{x\leq x_{m}}|\phi_{m}(x)+1|=0.

Since
U^{\prime\prime}(-1)=1
, for all
m
large enough we have

(5.7)

U(\phi_{m}(x))\geq\frac{1}{4}(\phi_{m}(x)+1)^{2}\qquad\text{for all }x\leq x_{m},

which proves part
1
.

Part
2
follows from
1
by symmetry.

In part
3
,
it suffices to prove that the conclusion holds for a subsequence of any subsequence.
We can thus assume that
\partial_{x}\phi_{m}\rightharpoonup\partial_{x}\phi_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and
\phi_{m}\to\phi_{0}
uniformly on every bounded interval.

From (
2.22
), we have

(5.8)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{x}\phi_{m}-\sqrt{2U(\phi_{m})}\big)^{2}\mathrm{\,d}x=0.

Restricting to bounded intervals and passing to the limit, we obtain
\partial_{x}\phi_{0}(x)=\sqrt{2U(\phi_{0}(x))}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, hence
\phi_{0}=H
.
Using again (
5.8
) restricted to bounded intervals,
we obtain
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\phi_{m}-H\|_{H^{1}(-R,R)}=0
for every
R>0
,
in particular
\lim_{m\to\infty}|E_{p}(\phi_{m};-R,R)-E_{p}(H;-R,R)|=0
.
Hence, there exists a sequence
R_{m}\to\infty
such that

(5.9)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(\|\phi_{m}-H\|_{H^{1}(-R_{m},R_{m})}+|E_{p}(\phi_{m};-R_{m},R_{m})-E_{p}(H;-R_{m},R_{m})|\big)=0.

Since
E_{p}(H;-R_{m},R_{m})\to M
and
E_{p}(\phi_{m})\to M
, we obtain

(5.10)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(E_{p}(\phi_{m};-\infty,-R_{m})+E_{p}(\phi_{m};R_{m},\infty)\big)=0.

Applying parts
1
and
2
, we obtain

(5.11)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(\|\phi_{m}+1\|_{H^{1}(-\infty,-R_{m})}+\|\phi_{m}-1\|_{H^{1}(R_{m},\infty)}\big)=0.

It is clear that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(\|H+1\|_{H^{1}(-\infty,-R_{m})}+\|H-1\|_{H^{1}(R_{m},\infty)}\big)=0
, thus (
5.9
) yields the conclusion.

In order to prove part
4
, we define a new sequence
\widetilde{\phi}_{m}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

by the formula, similar to (
3.3
),

(5.12)

\widetilde{\phi}_{m}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}-1&\text{for all }x\leq x_{m}-1,\\
-(x_{m}-x)+(1-x_{m}+x)\phi_{m}(x_{m})&\text{for all }x\in[x_{m}-1,x_{m}],\\
\phi_{m}(x)&\text{for all }x\in[x_{m},x_{m}^{\prime}],\\
(x-x_{m}^{\prime})+(1-x+x_{m}^{\prime})\phi_{m}(x_{m}^{\prime})&\text{for all }x\in[x_{m}^{\prime},x_{m}^{\prime}+1],\\
1&\text{for all }x\geq x_{m}^{\prime}+1.\end{cases}

Then
\widetilde{\phi}_{m}
satisfies the assumptions of part
3

and we obtain
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m}-H\|_{H^{1}}=0
.
In particular,
\widetilde{\phi}_{m}\to H
uniformly, hence
H(x_{m})\to-1
and
H(x_{m}^{\prime})\to 1
,
implying
x_{m}\to-\infty
and
x_{m}^{\prime}\to\infty
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.3
.

If
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a solution of (
1.10
) satisfying
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))=0
,
then Lemma
2.10
yields
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})=nM
.
Moreover, if
x_{0}(t),x_{1}(t),\ldots,x_{n}(t)
are any functions such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(a_{k}(t)-x_{k-1}(t)\big)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(x_{k}(t)-a_{k}(t)\big)=\infty\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

then we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi(t,x_{k}(t))=(-1)^{k}
for all
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
.

In the opposite direction, assume that
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink cluster according to Definition
1.1
.
For all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
t
sufficiently large, let
a_{k}(t)\in(x_{k-1}(t),x_{k}(t))

be such that
\phi(t,a_{k}(t))=0
. From (
2.22
) and (
2.23
), we obtain

(5.13)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi(t);x_{k-1}(t),x_{k}(t))\geq M,\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Thus, the condition
E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\leq nM
implies

(5.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}=0,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}E_{p}(\phi(t);x_{k-1}(t),x_{k}(t))=M,\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

(5.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(E_{p}(\phi(t);-\infty,x_{0}(t))+E_{p}(\phi(t);x_{n}(t),\infty)\big)=0.

Applying Lemma
5.1
, we obtain

(5.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}(a_{k}(t)-x_{k-1}(t))=\lim_{t\to\infty}(x_{k}(t)-a_{k}(t))=\infty\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

and

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\Big(\|\phi(t)\|_{H^{1}(-\infty,x_{0}(t))}+\|\phi(t)\|_{H^{1}(x_{n}(t),\infty)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\phi(t)-(-1)^{k}H(\cdot-a_{k}(t))\|_{H^{1}(x_{k-1}(t),x_{k}(t))}\Big)=0,

thus

(5.19)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t))\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a}(t))\big)=0.\qed

In particular, fixing
\vec{v}=0
in (
1.22
), we obtain
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))=0
.

5.2.
Asymptotically static solutions are kink clusters

The present section is devoted to a proof of Proposition
1.4
, which is inspired by some of the arguments in
[
7
,
33
]
.
Recall that we restrict our attention to the case
U(\phi)=\frac{1}{8}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
,
so that
\pm\boldsymbol{1}
and
\pm\boldsymbol{H}(\cdot-a)
are the only static states of (
1.10
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}
be a finite-energy solution of (
1.10
) such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0
.
If
t_{m}\to\infty
and
(a_{m})_{m}
is a sequence of real numbers such that

\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m},\cdot+a_{m})\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}
as
m\to\infty
,
then
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}
is a static state.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
be the solution of (
1.10
) for the initial data

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(0)=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}
.
By Proposition
3.2

5
, for all
s\in[0,1]

we have
\partial_{t}\phi(t_{m}+s,\cdot+a_{m})\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\widetilde{\phi}(s)

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, thus for all
s\in[0,1]
we have

(5.20)

\|\partial_{t}\widetilde{\phi}(s)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t_{m}+s,\cdot+a_{m})\|_{L^{2}}=\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t_{m}+s)\|_{L^{2}}=0,

implying that
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{0}
is a static state.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}
be a finite-energy solution of (
1.10
) such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0

and let
t_{m}\to\infty
.
After extraction of a subsequence, there exist

n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
,
\iota\in\{{-}1,1\}
and

\vec{a}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
such that

(5.21)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(\|\phi(t_{m})-\iota H(\vec{a}_{m})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a}_{m})\big)=0.

Proof.

Let
n
be the maximal natural number such that there exist
a subsequence of
t_{m}
, and sequences
a_{m,1},\ldots,a_{m,n}

satisfying

\lim_{m\to\infty}(a_{m,k+1}-a_{m,k})=\infty
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and

(5.22)

\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m},\cdot+a_{m,k})\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}\neq\pm\boldsymbol{1}\qquad\text{for all }k.

By Lemma
5.2
, each
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}
is a kink or an antikink. Upon adjusting the sequence
a_{m,k}
, we can assume

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}=\pm\boldsymbol{H}
for all
k
, and by an appropriate choice of
\iota

we can reduce to
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}={-}\boldsymbol{H}
.

We claim that
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}=(-1)^{k}\boldsymbol{H}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
This is true for
k=1
. Suppose
k
is such that
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k+1}
. Then there exists a sequence
x_{m}
such that

\lim_{m\to\infty}(x_{m}-a_{m,k})=\lim_{m\to\infty}(a_{m,k+1}-x_{m})=\infty
and
\phi(t_{m},x_{m})=0
for all
m
.
Extracting a subsequence, we can assume that
\phi(t_{m},\cdot+x_{m})

converges locally uniformly. The limit cannot be a vacuum,
contradicting the maximality of
n
and finishing the proof of the claim.

Suppose there exists a sequence
x_{m}
such that

(5.23)

\limsup_{m\to\infty}|\phi(t_{m},x_{m})-H(\vec{a}_{m};x_{m})|>0.

We then have
|a_{m,k}-x_{m}|\to\infty
,
thus
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m},\cdot+x_{m})
has (after extraction of a subsequence)
a non-constant weak limit, contradicting the maximality of
n
.
∎

The last auxiliary result which we need is the following
description of local in time stability of multikink configurations.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
and
\vec{a}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
be such that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho(\vec{a}_{m})=0
.
Let
\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}\in\mathcal{E}
for
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}

and let
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{E}_{1,(-1)^{n}}

be the solution of (
1.10
) for the initial data
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(0)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})+\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}
.

(1)

If
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0
, then

(5.24)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0.

(2)

If
\limsup_{m\to\infty}\|g_{m,0}\|_{H^{1}}<\infty
,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\dot{g}_{m,0}\|_{L^{2}}=0

and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|g_{m,0}\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
, then

(5.25)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})-\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0,

where
\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)
is the solution of the free linear Klein-Gordon
equation for the initial data
\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(0)=\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}
.

Proof.

Part
1
follows from part
2
and the fact that
\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}=\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}\|_{\mathcal{E}}
for all
t
.

In order to prove (
5.25
), we set
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(t)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})
and
\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)
. Then
\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)=(h_{m}(t),\partial_{t}h_{m}(t))
solves the equation

\partial_{t}^{2}h_{m}-\partial_{x}^{2}h_{m}+h_{m}=f_{m}
, where

(5.26)

\displaystyle f_{m}(t)\coloneqq

\displaystyle-\big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m})+g_{m}(t))-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))g_{m}(t)\big)

\displaystyle-\Big(U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H(\cdot-a_{k,m}))\Big)

\displaystyle-\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-1\big)g_{m}(t).

Since
\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(0)=0
, the standard energy estimate yields

(5.27)

\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\|f_{m}(s)\|_{L^{2}}\mathrm{\,d}s\bigg|\qquad\text{for all }t\in[-1,1].

By Gronwall’s inequality, it suffices to verify that

(5.28)

\|f_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}+\epsilon_{m},\qquad\text{
with }\lim_{m\to\infty}\epsilon_{m}=0.

The first line of (
5.26
) satisfies

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m})+g_{m}(t))-U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}

(5.30)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\min(\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}},\|g_{m}(t)^{2}\|_{L^{2}}).

If
\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}\geq 1
, then

\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|h_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}+\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\lesssim\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}.

If
\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq 1
, then

(5.31)

\displaystyle\|g_{m}(t)^{2}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{2}}

(5.32)

\displaystyle\leq(\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}+\|g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}})(\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}+\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m,0}\|_{\mathcal{E}})

(5.33)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\boldsymbol{h}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}+\|g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}.

The term on the second line of (
5.26
) is equal to
-\textrm{D}E_{p}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))
, thus, using (
2.69
), satisfies

(5.34)

\displaystyle\Big\|U^{\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}U^{\prime}(H(\cdot-a_{k,m}))\Big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{{-}\log\rho(\vec{a}_{m})}\rho(\vec{a}_{m}).

Finally, the third line of (
5.26
) satisfies

(5.35)

\displaystyle\big\|\big(U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-1\big)g_{m}(t)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|U^{\prime\prime}(H(\vec{a}_{m}))-1\|_{L^{2}}\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|g_{m}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Gathering the estimates above and using the fact that,
by explicit formulas for the free Klein-Gordon equation,
we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}\|g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
,
we obtain (
5.28
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.4
.

Without loss of generality, assume

(5.36)

\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi(t,x)=1\qquad\text{for all }t.

Suppose that either
n\coloneqq M^{-1}E(\boldsymbol{\phi})\notin\mathbb{N}
, or
there exists a sequence
t_{m}\to\infty
such that

(5.37)

\liminf_{m\to\infty}{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m}))>0,

where
{\bf d}
defined by (
1.22
) is the distance
to the set of
n
-kink configurations.
In both cases, for any
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\vec{a}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
we have

(5.38)

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\big(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m})-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+\rho(\vec{a}_{m})\big)>0.

By Lemma
5.3
, after extracting a subsequence,
we can assume that (
5.21
) holds
for some
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\vec{a}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
.
We have
\iota=1
due to (
5.36
).
We decompose

(5.39)

\boldsymbol{\phi}(t_{m}+s)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m})+\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(s),\qquad s\in[-1,1].

We claim that

(5.40)

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\inf_{s\in[-1,1]}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(s)\|_{\mathcal{E}}>0.

Otherwise, by Lemma
5.4

1
,
we would have
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0
,
contradicting (
5.38
).

Let
\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{E}
be the solution of the free
linear Klein-Gordon equation for the initial data
\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(0)=\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(0)
. By Lemma
5.4

2
,
we have

(5.41)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{s\in[-1,1]}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(s)-\boldsymbol{g}_{m,\mathrm{L}}(s)\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0,

thus

(5.42)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{s\in[-1,1]}\|\partial_{t}g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(s)\|_{L^{2}}=0,

(5.43)

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to\infty}\inf_{s\in[-1,1]}\|g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(s)\|_{H^{1}}>0,

the last inequality following from (
5.40
).
It is well-known that such behaviour is impossible
for the free Klein-Gordon equation.
For instance, it contradicts the identity

(5.44)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{t}g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(1,x)g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(1,x)-\partial_{t}g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(-1,x)g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(-1,x)\big)\mathrm{\,d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big((\partial_{t}g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t,x))^{2}-(\partial_{x}g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t,x))^{2}-g_{m,\mathrm{L}}(t,x)^{2}\big)\mathrm{\,d}x\mathrm{\,d}t,

obtained by multiplying the equation by
g_{m,\mathrm{L}}
and integrating in space-time.
Hence, (
5.37
) is impossible.
∎

6.
Main order asymptotic behavior of kink clusters

The current section is devoted to the analysis of the approximate
n
-body problem
associated with a kink cluster motion, obtained in Lemma
4.7
.

6.1.
Linear algebra results

We gather here a few auxiliary results needed later.

Definition 6.1
.

For any
n\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
, the (positive) discrete
Dirichlet Laplacian
\Delta^{(n)}=(\Delta_{jk}^{(n)})_{j,k=1}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}
is defined by

\Delta^{(n)}_{kk}=2
for
k=1,\ldots,n-1
,

\Delta^{(n)}_{k,k+1}=\Delta^{(n)}_{k+1,k}=-1

for
k=1,\ldots,n-2
and
\Delta^{(n)}_{j,k}=0
for
|j-k|\geq 2
.

We denote

(6.1)

\vec{1}\coloneqq(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\qquad\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}),\ \sigma_{k}\coloneqq\frac{k(n-k)}{2}.

We have

(6.2)

\Delta^{(n)}\vec{\sigma}=\Big(k(n-k)-\frac{1}{2}((k-1)(n-k+1)+(k+1)(n-k-1))\Big)_{k=1}^{n-1}=\vec{1}.

We also denote

(6.3)

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{z}=0\},

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{1}}=\frac{12}{(n+1)n(n-1)},

(6.5)

\displaystyle P_{\sigma}\vec{y}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{y}-\frac{\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{y}}{\|\vec{\sigma}\|^{2}}\vec{\sigma}\quad\text{(the orthogonal projection of $\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}$ on $\Pi$)},

(6.6)

\displaystyle P_{1}\vec{y}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{y}-\mu_{0}(\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{y})\vec{1}\quad\text{(the projection of $\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}$ on $\Pi$ along the direction $\vec{1}$)}.

Observe that

(6.7)

P_{1}\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y}=\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y}-\mu_{0}(\vec{\sigma}\cdot(\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y}))\vec{1}=\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y}-\mu_{0}(\vec{1}\cdot\vec{y})\vec{1}.

Lemma 6.2
.

The matrix
\Delta^{(n)}
is positive definite. There exists
\mu_{1}>0
such that for all
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}

(6.8)

\vec{y}\cdot(P_{1}\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y})=\vec{y}\cdot(\Delta^{(n)}\vec{y})-\mu_{0}(\vec{1}\cdot\vec{y})^{2}\geq\mu_{1}|P_{\sigma}\vec{y}|^{2}.

Proof.

Since
\vec{\sigma}
and
\vec{1}
are not orthogonal, it suffices to prove (
6.8
).

Consider the matrix
\widetilde{\Delta}=(\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk})
given by
\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk}\coloneqq 2\delta_{jk}+\mu_{0}-\Delta_{jk}
.
The desired inequality is equivalent to

(6.9)

\vec{y}\cdot(\widetilde{\Delta}\vec{y})\leq 2|\vec{y}|^{2}-\mu_{1}|P_{\sigma}\vec{y}|^{2}.

We have

(6.10)

\widetilde{\Delta}\vec{\sigma}=2\vec{\sigma}+\mu_{0}(\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma})\vec{1}-\Delta^{(n)}\vec{\sigma}=2\vec{\sigma}.

The matrix
\widetilde{\Delta}
and the vector
\vec{\sigma}
have strictly positive entries.
By the Perron-Frobenius theorem, the largest eigenvalue of
\widetilde{\Delta}
equals
2
and is simple, which implies (
6.9
).
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

(1)

The function

(6.11)

\Pi\owns\vec{z}\mapsto\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}\in(0,\infty)

has a unique critical point
\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}
, which is its global minimum.

(2)

There exist
C>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
\vec{z}\in\Pi
and
\delta\leq\delta_{0}

(6.12)

|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|\leq\delta\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\vec{z}-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}|\leq C\delta.

(3)

There exists
C>0
such that for all
\vec{z}\in\Pi

(6.13)

C^{-1}|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|^{2}\leq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}\big(\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}\big)\leq C|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|^{2}.

Proof.

The function
\vec{z}\mapsto\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}
,
defined on the hyperplane
\Pi
, is strictly convex and tends to
\infty
as
|\vec{z}|\to\infty
, thus it has no critical points other than its global minimum.
By the Lagrange multiplier method, this unique critical point
\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}
is determined by

(6.14)

e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}\vec{\sigma}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}=-\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}\vec{\sigma})=-\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}})\vec{1}-\log\vec{\sigma},

where
\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}\coloneqq\exp(-\mu_{0}\vec{\sigma}\cdot\log\vec{\sigma})
is the unique value for which
\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}\in\Pi
.

We can rewrite the condition
|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|\leq\delta\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}
as follows:

(6.15)

e^{-\vec{z}}=\lambda\vec{\sigma}+\vec{u},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \vec{u}\in\Pi,\ |\vec{u}|\leq\delta\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}.

We thus have
\lambda=|\vec{\sigma}|^{-2}\vec{\sigma}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}
, which implies
\lambda\simeq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}
, so

(6.16)

|{-}\vec{z}-\log(\lambda\vec{\sigma})|=|\log(\lambda\vec{\sigma}+\vec{u})-\log(\lambda\vec{\sigma})|\lesssim\frac{|\vec{u}|}{\lambda}\lesssim\delta.

By (
6.14
) and the triangle inequality, we obtain

(6.17)

|\vec{z}-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}+(\log\lambda-\log\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}})\vec{1}|\lesssim\delta.

Taking the inner product with
\vec{\sigma}
, we get
|\log\lambda-\log\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}|\lesssim\delta
, which proves
2
.

Finally, we prove
3
.
If
|\vec{z}-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}|\leq 1
, then
\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}\simeq 1

and
\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}\simeq|\vec{z}-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}|^{2}
, thus it suffices to verify that
|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|\simeq|z-z_{\mathrm{cr}}|
.
The inequality
\lesssim
follows from
P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}=0

and the mean value theorem. The inequality
\gtrsim
follows from
2
.
Suppose now that
|\vec{z}-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}|\geq 1
. We then have

\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}\simeq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}

and, invoking again
2
,
|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}}|\simeq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}}
.
∎

6.2.
Analysis of the
n
-body problem

Let
\vec{y}(t)=(y_{1}(t),\ldots,y_{n-1}(t))
be defined by

(6.18)

y_{k}(t)\coloneqq a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t),\qquad k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

and let

(6.19)

y_{\min}(t)\coloneqq\min_{1\leq k<n}y_{k}(t).

We denote

(6.20)

\widetilde{\rho}(t)\coloneqq\rho(\vec{a}(t))=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{-y_{k}(t)},

which (up to a constant) is the potential energy of the ODE system (
1.42
).
From (
4.48
), we have

(6.21)

\|\boldsymbol{g}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}+|\vec{v}(t)|^{2}\lesssim\widetilde{\rho}(t)\simeq e^{-y_{\min}(t)}.

Let
\vec{q}(t)=(q_{1}(t),\ldots,q_{n-1}(t))

be defined by

(6.22)

q_{k}(t)\coloneqq M^{-1}\big(p_{k+1}(t)-p_{k}(t)\big).

By (
4.33
) and.(
4.51
), we have

(6.23)

|{\vec{y}\,}^{\prime}(t)-\vec{q}(t)|\lesssim e^{-y_{\min}(t)}.

By (
4.51
) and (
6.21
), we have

(6.24)

|\vec{q}(t)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}y_{\min}(t)}.

Let

(6.25)

A\coloneqq\frac{\kappa\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{M}}.

By Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
4.7
, we have

(6.26)

{\vec{q}\,}^{\prime}(t)=-A^{2}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}(t)}+O(y_{\min}(t)^{-1}e^{-y_{\min}(t)}),

where
\Delta^{(n)}
is the matrix of the (positive) discrete Dirichlet Laplacian, see Definition
6.1
,
and we denote
e^{-\vec{y}(t)}\coloneqq(e^{-y_{1}(t)},\ldots,e^{-y_{n-1}(t)})

as explained in Section
1.6
.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
n>1
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink
n
-cluster, then

(6.27)

\sup_{t\geq 0}|y_{\min}(t)-2\log t|<\infty.

Proof.

We first prove that
\sup_{t\geq T_{0}}y_{\min}(t)-2\log t<\infty
.
By (
4.32
), we have
|y_{k}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}y_{\min}(t)}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Therefore,
y_{\min}
is a locally Lipschitz function, satisfying

(6.28)

|y_{\min}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}y_{\min}(t)},\qquad\text{for almost all }t\geq 0.

By the Chain Rule,
|(e^{\frac{1}{2}y_{\min}(t)})^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
almost everywhere.
After integration, we obtain
y_{\min}(t)-2\log t\lesssim 1
.

Now we prove that
\sup_{t\geq 0}y_{\min}(t)-2\log t>-\infty
.
Let
c_{0}>0
be small enough and consider the function

(6.29)

\beta(t)\coloneqq\vec{q}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{y}(t)}-c_{0}e^{-\frac{3}{2}y_{\min}(t)}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}q_{k}(t)e^{-y_{k}(t)}-c_{0}e^{-\frac{3}{2}y_{\min}(t)}.

Using (
6.23
) and (
6.26
) and
|q_{k}(t)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}y_{\min}(t)}\ll y_{\min}^{-1}
, we obtain

(6.30)

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(t)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}q_{k}(t)^{2}e^{-y_{k}(t)}-A^{2}e^{-\vec{y}(t)}\cdot(\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}(t)})

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}c_{0}y_{\min}^{\prime}(t)e^{-\frac{3}{2}y_{\min}(t)}+O(y_{\min}(t)^{-1}e^{-2y_{\min}(t)}).

By Lemma
6.2
, there is
c_{1}>0
such that for all
t
we have

e^{-\vec{y}(t)}\cdot(\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}(t)})\geq c_{1}e^{-2y_{\min}(t)}
.
If we recall (
6.28
), we obtain that
\beta
is a decreasing function
if
c_{0}
is sufficiently small. By assumption,
\lim_{t\to\infty}y_{\min}(t)=\infty
, which, together with (
6.24
), implies
\lim_{t\to\infty}\beta(t)=0
,
hence
\beta(t)\geq 0
for all
t\geq T_{0}
, in other words

(6.31)

\vec{q}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{y}(t)}\geq c_{0}e^{-\frac{3}{2}y_{\min}(t)}.

Consider now the function
\widetilde{\rho}(t)
defined by (
6.20
).
From (
6.23
) and (
6.31
) we get, perhaps modifying
c_{0}
,

(6.32)

\widetilde{\rho}\,^{\prime}(t)=-\vec{q}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{y}(t)}+O(e^{-2y_{\min}})\leq-c_{0}\widetilde{\rho}(t)^{\frac{3}{2}}\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\big((\widetilde{\rho}(t))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2},

so that, after integrating in time,
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\lesssim t^{-2}
, yielding the conclusion.
∎

We have thus obtained upper and lower bounds on the minimal weighted distance between neighboring kinks.
Our next goal is to obtain an upper bound on the
maximal
distance.
From the lower bound, by integrating the modulation inequalities we get
|\vec{y}(t)|\lesssim\log t
.
However, because of exponential interactions, our analysis requires an estimate up to an
additive
constant.

Remark 6.5
.

At this point, by means of energy estimates, we could improve bounds on the error to
\|\boldsymbol{g}(t)\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq t^{-2+\epsilon}
for
t\gg 1
.
However, such information does not seem to trivialise the analysis of the ODE,
which is not immediate even in the absence of
any
error terms.

Lemma 6.6
.

If
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink
n
-cluster and
\widetilde{\rho}
is defined by (
6.20
), then

(6.33)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{2}A^{2}t^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t)\geq\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma}.

Proof.

The idea is to estimate from below the kinetic energy.

From the lower bound on the distance between the kinks, there exists
C
such that for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
t\geq T_{0}
we have

(6.34)

|a_{n+1-k}(t)-a_{k}(t)|\geq 2|n+1-2k|\log t-C,

in particular

(6.35)

\int_{T_{0}}^{t}\big(|a_{n+1-k}^{\prime}(s)|+|a_{k}^{\prime}(s)|\big)\mathrm{\,d}s\geq 2|n+1-2k|\log t-C.

By Lemma
4.7
and Lemma
6.4
, we have

(6.36)

\int_{T_{0}}^{\infty}|M^{-1}\vec{p}(s)-{\vec{a}\,}^{\prime}(s)|\mathrm{\,d}s<\infty,

thus (
6.35
) yields

(6.37)

M^{-1}\int_{T_{0}}^{t}(|p_{k}(s)|+|p_{n+1-k}(s)|)\mathrm{\,d}s\geq 2|n+1-2k|\log t-C.

Multiplying by
|n+1-2k|
and taking the sum in
k
, we obtain

(6.38)

M^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{T_{0}}^{t}|(n+1-2k)p_{k}(s)|\geq\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}\bigg)\log t-C,

hence, as
t\to\infty
, the quantity

(6.39)

\int_{T_{0}}^{t}\Big(s\sum_{k=1}^{n}|(n+1-2k)p_{k}(s)|-M\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

is bounded from below, which implies in particular

(6.40)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\,t\sum_{k=1}^{n}|(n+1-2k)p_{k}(t)|\geq M\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields

(6.41)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\,t^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}|p_{k}(t)|^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}}\bigg(\limsup_{t\to\infty}\,t\sum_{k=1}^{n}|(n+1-2k)p_{k}(t)|\bigg)^{2}\geq\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}\Big)^{2},

thus

(6.42)

\frac{1}{4M^{2}}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\,t^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}|p_{k}(t)|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}=\frac{(n-1)n(n+1)}{12}=\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma}.

By (
4.48
) and (
4.51
), we have

(6.43)

\frac{1}{4M^{2}}|\vec{p}(t)|^{2}\leq\big(\kappa^{2}M^{-1}+o(1)\big)\widetilde{\rho}(t)=\Big(\frac{1}{2}A^{2}+o(1)\Big)\widetilde{\rho}(t),

thus (
6.42
) implies (
6.33
).
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

If
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink
n
-cluster, then there exists an increasing sequence
(t_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
,

\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}=\infty
, such that

(6.44)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big|\vec{y}(t_{m})-\big(2\log(At_{m})\vec{1}-\log(2\vec{\sigma})\big)\big|=0.

Proof.

Set
\overline{\ell}\coloneqq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}A^{2}t^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t)
.
Lemma
6.6
yields
\overline{\ell}\geq\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma}

and Lemma
6.4
yields
\overline{\ell}<\infty
.
Let
t_{m}
be an increasing sequence such that
\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}=\infty
and

(6.45)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}A^{2}t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})=\overline{\ell}.

We will prove that
(t_{m})
satisfies (
6.44
), in particular
\overline{\ell}=\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma}
.
In order to make the formulas shorter, we denote
\vec{y}_{m}\coloneqq\vec{y}(t_{m})
and
\vec{q}_{m}\coloneqq\vec{q}(t_{m})
.

Let
0<\epsilon\ll 1
. The idea is to deduce (
6.44
)
by analysing the evolution of
\vec{y}(t)
for
(1-\epsilon)t_{m}\leq t\leq(1+\epsilon)t_{m}
.
In the computation which follows, the asymptotic notation
o
,
O
,
\ll
etc.
is used for claims about the asymptotic behaviour of various quantities for
m
large enough and
\epsilon
small enough.

Lemma
6.4
yields
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\lesssim t^{-2}
, hence
|{\vec{y}\,}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim t^{-1}
. We thus obtain

(6.46)

|\vec{y}(t)-\vec{y}_{m}|\lesssim\epsilon,\qquad\text{for all }(1-\epsilon)t_{m}\leq t\leq(1+\epsilon)t_{m},

which implies

(6.47)

|e^{-\vec{y}(t)}-e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}|\lesssim\epsilon t_{m}^{-2},\qquad\text{for all }(1-\epsilon)t_{m}\leq t\leq(1+\epsilon)t_{m}.

Using this bound and integrating (
6.26
) in time, we get

(6.48)

\vec{q}(t)=\vec{q}_{m}-(t-t_{m})A^{2}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+o(\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}),\qquad\text{for all }(1-\epsilon)t_{m}\leq t\leq(1+\epsilon)t_{m}.

Integrating (
6.23
), we get

(6.49)

\displaystyle\vec{y}(t)

\displaystyle=\vec{y}_{m}+\int_{t_{m}}^{t}\vec{q}(\tau)\mathrm{\,d}\tau+O(\epsilon t_{m}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\vec{y}_{m}+\int_{t_{m}}^{t}\big(\vec{q}_{m}-(\tau-t_{m})A^{2}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)\mathrm{\,d}\tau+o(\epsilon^{2})+O(\epsilon t_{m}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\vec{y}_{m}+(t-t_{m})\vec{q}_{m}-\frac{1}{2}(t-t_{m})^{2}A^{2}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+o(\epsilon^{2})+O(\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}).

By taking
m
large enough, we can have
t_{m}^{-1}\ll\epsilon
. The bound above allows us to compute the asymptotic expansion
of
t^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t)
up to order
o(\epsilon^{2})
. Recalling from Section
1.6
our notation for component-wise operations on vectors, we can write

(6.50)

e^{-\vec{y}(t)}=e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\Big(1-(t-t_{m})\vec{q}_{m}+\frac{1}{2}(t-t_{m})^{2}\big((\vec{q}_{m})^{2}+A^{2}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)\Big)+o(\epsilon^{2}t_{m}^{-2}),

thus

(6.51)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})-(t-t_{m})\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(t-t_{m})^{2}\big((\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+A^{2}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\cdot\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)+o(\epsilon^{2}t_{m}^{-2}).

In particular, we have

(6.52)

\displaystyle((1\pm\epsilon)t_{m})^{2}\widetilde{\rho}((1+\epsilon)t_{m})=t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})\pm\epsilon\big(2t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})-t_{m}^{3}\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\Big(t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})-2t_{m}^{3}\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+\frac{1}{2}t_{m}^{4}\big((\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+A^{2}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\cdot\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)\Big)+o(\epsilon^{2}).

From this estimate and the definition of the sequence
(t_{m})
we deduce that

(6.53)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big(2t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})-t_{m}^{3}\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)=0

and

(6.54)

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\Big(t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})-2t_{m}^{3}\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+\frac{1}{2}t_{m}^{4}\big((\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}+A^{2}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\cdot\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)\Big)\leq 0.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

(6.55)

\big(t_{m}^{3}\vec{q}_{m}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)^{2}\leq t_{m}^{2}\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}t_{m}^{4}(\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}=t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})t_{m}^{4}(\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}},

thus (
6.53
) yields

(6.56)

\liminf_{m\to\infty}t_{m}^{4}(\vec{q}_{m})^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\geq 4\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m}).

Injecting this estimate to (
6.54
) and using again (
6.53
), we obtain

(6.57)

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\big(t_{m}^{4}A^{2}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\cdot\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}\big)\leq 2\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m}),

hence, by Lemma
6.2
,

(6.58)

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\big(A^{2}\mu_{0}(t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m}))^{2}+A^{2}\mu_{1}|t_{m}^{2}P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}|^{2}\big)\leq 2\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m}).

Recalling that
\lim_{m\to\infty}t_{m}^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})\geq 2A^{-2}\mu_{0}^{-1}
, we obtain that in the last inequality
there is in fact equality and, additionally,
\lim_{m\to\infty}|t_{m}^{2}P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}|=0
.
In other words,
e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}=\lambda_{m}\vec{\sigma}+o(t_{m}^{-2})
. The coefficient
\lambda_{m}
is determined by

(6.59)

2(At_{m})^{-2}\mu_{0}^{-1}+o(t_{m}^{-2})=\widetilde{\rho}(t_{m})=\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}=\lambda_{m}\vec{1}\cdot\vec{\sigma}+o(t_{m}^{-2}),

thus
\lambda_{m}=2(At_{m})^{-2}+o(t_{m}^{-2})
, so
e^{-\vec{y}_{m}}=2(At_{m})^{-2}\vec{\sigma}+o(t_{m}^{-2})
,
which yields (
6.44
) after taking logarithms.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

From the proof, one can see that
\lim_{m\to\infty}|t_{m}\vec{q}(t_{m})-2\times\vec{1}|=0
.
Below, we conclude the modulation analysis without using this information.

We decompose

(6.60)

\begin{gathered}\vec{y}(t)=r(t)\vec{1}+\vec{z}(t),\qquad\vec{z}(t)\coloneqq P_{1}\vec{y}(t),\\
\vec{q}(t)=b(t)\vec{1}+\vec{w}(t),\qquad\vec{w}(t)\coloneqq P_{1}\vec{q}(t).\end{gathered}

We thus have

(6.61)

\begin{gathered}{\vec{y}\,}^{\prime}(t)=r^{\prime}(t)\vec{1}+{\vec{z}\,}^{\prime}(t),\qquad{\vec{z}\,}^{\prime}(t)=P_{1}\vec{y}^{\prime}(t),\\
{\vec{q}\,}^{\prime}(t)=b^{\prime}(t)\vec{1}+{\vec{w}\,}^{\prime}(t),\qquad{\vec{w}\,}^{\prime}(t)=P_{1}{\vec{q}\,}^{\prime}(t).\end{gathered}

We denote
z_{\min}(t)\coloneqq\min_{1\leq k\leq n-1}z_{j}(t)
. We note that
z_{\min}(t)\leq 0
and
y_{\min}(t)=r(t)+z_{\min}(t)
.
Observe also that
|z_{\min}(t)|\simeq|\vec{z}(t)|
, since both quantities are norms on the hyperplane
\Pi
, and that
e^{-z_{\min}(t)}\simeq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}
. Hence,
Lemma
6.3

3
yields

(6.62)

\displaystyle e^{-r(t)}|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}(t)}|^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq e^{z_{\min}(t)-y_{\min}(t)}\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}(\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}})

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-y_{\min}(t)}(\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}).

Taking the inner product of (
6.60
) and (
6.61
) with
\vec{\sigma}
, and using (
6.23
)–(
6.26
),
we obtain

(6.63)

\displaystyle|r^{\prime}(t)-b(t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-r(t)}e^{-z_{\min}(t)},

\displaystyle|b^{\prime}(t)+\mu_{0}A^{2}e^{-r(t)}\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim y_{\min}(t)^{-1}e^{-r(t)}e^{-z_{\min}(t)}.

From this, again using (
6.23
)–(
6.26
), we deduce

(6.64)

\displaystyle|{\vec{z}\,}^{\prime}(t)-\vec{w}(t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-r(t)}e^{-z_{\min}(t)},

\displaystyle|{\vec{w}\,}^{\prime}(t)+e^{-r(t)}A^{2}P_{1}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{z}(t)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim y_{\min}(t)^{-1}e^{-r(t)}e^{-z_{\min}(t)}.

In Lemma
6.7
, we proved that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}|\vec{z}(t)-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}|=0
.
Our next goal is to improve this information to continuous time convergence, in other words we prove a no-return lemma.

Proposition 6.9
.

If
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a kink
n
-cluster, then

(6.65)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(\big|\vec{y}(t)-\big(2\log(At)\vec{1}-\log(2\vec{\sigma})\big)\big|+\big|t\vec{q}(t)-2\times\vec{1}\big|\big)=0.

Proof.

Step 1.

Consider the functions

(6.66)

\xi(t)\coloneqq\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}-\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}},\qquad\zeta(t)\coloneqq\vec{w}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}-c_{0}t^{-1}\xi(t),

where
c_{0}>0
will be chosen below.
We know that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\xi(t)=0
, and we will improve this to
\lim_{t\to\infty}\xi(t)=0
.

We claim that for every
\epsilon>0
there exist
c_{0}=c_{0}(\epsilon)>0
and
t_{0}=t_{0}(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}

(6.67)

\xi(t)\geq\epsilon\ \text{and}\ \zeta(t)\leq 0\ \Rightarrow\ \zeta^{\prime}(t)\leq 0.

Indeed, applying successively (
6.64
), (
6.8
) and
(
6.62
), we get

(6.68)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{w}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}

\displaystyle\leq-\vec{w}(t)^{2}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}-e^{-r(t)}A^{2}e^{-\vec{z}(t)}\cdot(P_{1}\Delta^{(n)}e^{-\vec{z}(t)})+o(e^{-r(t)}e^{-2z_{\min}(t)})

\displaystyle\lesssim-e^{-r(t)}|P_{\sigma}e^{-\vec{z}(t)}|^{2}+o(e^{-r(t)}e^{-2z_{\min}(t)})\lesssim-e^{-y_{\min}(t)}(\xi(t)+o(e^{-z_{\min}(t)})).

Now observe that
\xi(t)\geq\epsilon
implies
\xi(t)\geq c_{1}e^{-z_{\min}(t)}
,
where
c_{1}=c_{1}(\epsilon)>0
. Hence, for
t
large enough

(6.69)

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\vec{w}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}\lesssim-e^{-y_{\min}(t)}\xi(t)\lesssim-t^{-2}\xi(t).

We have
-(c_{0}t^{-1}\xi(t))^{\prime}=c_{0}t^{-2}\xi(t)-c_{0}t^{-1}\xi^{\prime}(t)
.
If
c_{0}
is small enough, then (
6.69
) allows to absorb the first term.
Regarding the second term, (
6.64
)
and the assumption
\zeta(t)\leq 0
yield

(6.70)

-\xi^{\prime}(t)=\vec{w}(t)\cdot e^{-\vec{z}(t)}+O(e^{-r(t)}e^{-2z_{\min}(t)})\leq c_{0}t^{-1}\xi(t)+O(t^{-2}\xi(t)).

For
c_{0}
small enough and
t
large enough, the term
{-}c_{0}t^{-1}\xi^{\prime}(t)
can thus be absorbed as well,
which finishes Step 1.

Step 2.

We are ready to prove that

(6.71)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\vec{z}(t)=\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}.

Suppose this is false, so there exists
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and a sequence
t_{m}\to\infty
such that

(6.72)

\xi(t_{m})=\epsilon_{0},\qquad\xi^{\prime}(t_{m})\geq 0

(this is not the same sequence as in Lemma
6.7
,
but we denote it by the same symbol in order to simplify the notation).
Take
0<\epsilon\ll\epsilon_{0}
,
c_{0}=c_{0}(\epsilon)
and
m
large (depending on
\epsilon
).
Using (
6.64
), we have

(6.73)

\zeta(t_{m})=-\xi^{\prime}(t_{m})-c_{0}t_{m}^{-1}\xi(t_{m})+O(e^{-r(t_{m})}e^{-2z_{\min}(t_{m})})\lesssim-c_{0}\epsilon_{0}t_{m}^{-1}.

Let
\tau_{m}
be the first time
\tau_{m}\geq t_{m}
such that
\xi(\tau_{m})=\epsilon
.
On the time interval
[t_{m},\tau_{m}]
we have
\xi(t)\geq\epsilon
, so Step 2. implies that
\zeta
is decreasing,
provided
m
is large enough, thus
\zeta(\tau_{m})\lesssim-c_{0}\epsilon_{0}t_{m}^{-1}\leq-c_{0}\epsilon_{0}\tau_{m}^{-1}
.
On the other hand,
\xi^{\prime}(\tau_{m})\leq 0
, which, by the same computation as above, leads to
\zeta(\tau_{m})\gtrsim-c_{0}\epsilon\tau_{m}^{-1}
, a contradiction.

Step 3.

We claim that

(6.74)

\lim_{t\to\infty}t|\vec{w}(t)|=0.

We use a Tauberian argument. From (
6.64
), we have
|{\vec{z}\,}^{\prime}(t)-\vec{w}(t)|+|{\vec{w}\,}^{\prime}(t)|\leq Ct^{-2}
for all
t
.
If there existed
\epsilon>0
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and a sequence
t_{m}\to\infty
such that
w_{k}(t_{m})\geq\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}
for all
m
,
then we would have
w_{k}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}
for all
t\in\big[t_{m},\big(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\big)t_{m}\big]
, hence for
m
large enough
z_{k}^{\prime}(t)\geq\frac{1}{4}\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}
for all
t\in\big[t_{m},\big(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\big)t_{m}\big]
,
contradicting the convergence of
z_{k}(t)
as
t\to\infty
. The case
w_{k}(t_{m})\leq{-}\epsilon t_{m}^{-1}
is similar.

From (
6.14
), we have
\mu_{0}\vec{1}\cdot e^{-\vec{z}_{\mathrm{cr}}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}
.
Thus, (
6.63
) and (
6.71
) yield

(6.75)

|r^{\prime}(t)-b(t)|\lesssim e^{-r(t)},\qquad|b^{\prime}(t)+A^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}e^{-r(t)}|\ll e^{-r(t)}.

From Lemma
6.4
, (
6.60
) and (
6.71
) we have
\sup_{t\geq 0}|r(t)-2\log t|<\infty
. Furthermore, (
6.24
) and (
6.74
) yield

\sup_{t\geq 0}t|b(t)|<\infty
. Hence,

(6.76)

\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}b(t)^{2}-A^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}e^{-r(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle=b(t)\big(b^{\prime}(t)+A^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}e^{-r(t)}\big)

\displaystyle+A^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}(r^{\prime}(t)-b(t))e^{-r(t)}=o(t^{-3}),

and an integration in
t
leads to

(6.77)

\big(b(t)-A\sqrt{2\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r(t)}\big)\big(b(t)+A\sqrt{2\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r(t)}\big)=b(t)^{2}-2A^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}e^{-r(t)}=o(t^{-2}).

The second bound in (
6.75
) yields

b^{\prime}(t)<0
for all
t
large enough. Since
\lim_{t\to\infty}b(t)=0
,
we have
b(t)>0
for all
t
large enough, hence (
6.75
) and (
6.77
) yield

(6.78)

r^{\prime}(t)-A\sqrt{2\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r(t)}=b(t)-A\sqrt{2\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r(t)}+o(t^{-1})=o(t^{-1}),

which implies

(6.79)

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\big(e^{\frac{1}{2}r(t)}\big)=A\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}{2}}+o(1),

thus

(6.80)

e^{\frac{1}{2}r(t)}=tA\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}}{2}}\big(1+o(1)\big)

and after taking the logarithm we finally obtain

(6.81)

r(t)=2\log(At)+\log\big(\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{cr}}\big)+o(1).

Invoking again (
6.78
), we also have
b(t)=2t^{-1}+o(1)
.
Hence, (
6.65
) follows from (
6.60
), (
6.14
),
(
6.71
) and (
6.74
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

The bound on
a_{k+1}(t)-a_{k}(t)
follows directly
from (
6.65
), and the definitions of
A
and
\vec{\sigma}
.

Let
\overline{a}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}(t)
.
Then for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, (
6.65
) implies

(6.82)

a_{k}^{\prime}(t)=\overline{a}\,^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j}(a_{k}^{\prime}(t)-a_{j}^{\prime}(t))=\overline{a}\,^{\prime}(t)+(2k-n-1)t^{-1}+o(t^{-1}),

hence

(6.83)

|\vec{a}\,^{\prime}(t)|^{2}=(\overline{a}\,^{\prime}(t))^{2}+t^{-2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}+o(t^{-2}).

An explicit computation yields

(6.84)

4\kappa^{2}\widetilde{\rho}(t)=Mt^{-2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n+1-2k)^{2}+o(t^{-2}).

Applying (
4.48
), we obtain

(6.85)

\lim_{t\to\infty}t\big(\|\partial_{t}g(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|g(t)\|_{H^{1}}+|\overline{a}\,^{\prime}(t)|\big)=0.

The required bound on
a_{k}^{\prime}(t)
follows from the last inequality and (
6.82
).
∎

7.
Existence of a kink
n
-cluster for prescribed initial positions

The present section is devoted to a proof of Theorem
2
.
The case
n=1
is clear, hence we assume
n>1
.
We will use the following consequence of Brouwer’s fixed point theorem,
known as the Poincaré–Miranda theorem.

Theorem 7.1
.

[
51
]

Let
L_{1}<L_{2}
,
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\vec{\Psi}=(\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{d}):[L_{1},L_{2}]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a continuous map such that
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\Psi_{k}(\vec{x})\leq y_{k}
for all
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in[L_{1},L_{2}]^{d}
such that
x_{k}=L_{1}
,

•

\Psi_{k}(\vec{x})\geq y_{k}
for all
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in[L_{1},L_{2}]^{d}
such that
x_{k}=L_{2}
.

Then there exists
\vec{x}\in[L_{1},L_{2}]^{d}
such that
\vec{\Psi}(\vec{x})=\vec{y}
.

Remark 7.2
.

The result is often stated with
L_{1}=-1
,
L_{2}=1
and
\vec{y}=0
,
which can be achieved by a straightforward change of coordinates.

Lemma 7.3
.

There exist
L_{0},C_{0}>0
such that the following is true.
Let
L\geq L_{0}
and
\vec{a}_{0}=(a_{0,1},\ldots,a_{0,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
a_{0,k+1}-a_{0,k}\geq L
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
For any
T\geq 0
there exists
(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that the solution
\boldsymbol{\phi}
of (
1.10
) with

(7.1)

\boldsymbol{\phi}(T)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})

has energy
nM
and satisfies
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))+\boldsymbol{g}(t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
, where

(1)

\boldsymbol{g}(t)
satisfies (
4.3
),

(2)

\rho(t)\leq C_{0}/(e^{L}+t^{2})
for all
t\in[0,T]
, where
\rho(t)
is defined by (
2.36
),

(3)

\vec{a}(0)=\vec{a}_{0}
.

Proof.

Step 1.
(Preliminary observations.)
In order to have
\vec{a}(0)=\vec{a}_{0}
,
it suffices to guarantee that
a_{k+1}(0)-a_{k}(0)=a_{0,k+1}-a_{0,k}
,
and apply a translation if needed.
Set
y_{0,k}\coloneqq a_{0,k+1}-a_{0,k}
and let
0<L_{1}<L_{2}
be chosen later
(depending on
T
and
\vec{y}_{0}
).
The idea is to construct an appropriate function
\vec{\Psi}:[L_{1},L_{2}]^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
7.1
.

For given
\vec{y}_{T}\in[L_{1},L_{2}]^{n-1}
, let
\vec{a}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
a_{T,k+1}-a_{T,k}=y_{T,k}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

and
\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{T,k}=0

(the last condition is a matter of choice, we could just as well impose
a_{T,1}=0

or any condition of similar kind), so that
\vec{a}_{T}=\vec{a}_{T}(\vec{y}_{T})
is continuous.
Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}=\boldsymbol{\phi}(T,\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})
be the solution of (
1.10
)
for the data (
7.1
) at time
T
, and let
T_{0}\geq 0
be the minimal time
such that
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))\leq\eta_{0}
for all
t\in[T_{0},T]
(we set
T_{0}=0

if
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))\leq\eta_{0}
for all
t\in[0,T]
).
We thus have well-defined modulation parameters
\vec{a}(t)
and
\vec{v}(t)
for all
t\in[T_{0},T]
.
We define
\vec{p}(t)
,
\vec{y}(t)
,
y_{\min}(t)
,
\widetilde{\rho}(t)
and
\vec{q}(t)
as in Sections
4
and
6
.

Step 2.
(Choice of
\vec{v}_{T}
.)
Let
y_{T,\min}\coloneqq\min_{1\leq k<n}y_{T,k}
.
We define
\vec{v}_{T}=\vec{v}_{T}(\vec{a}_{T})
as the unique vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(1)

\sum_{k=1}^{n}v_{T,k}=0
,

(2)

there exists
\lambda>0
such that
v_{T,k+1}-v_{T,k}=\lambda\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{T})}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,

(3)

E\big(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})\big)=nM
.

Conditions
1
and
2
are equivalent to

(7.2)

v_{T,k}=\frac{2k-n-1}{2}\lambda\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{T})},\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Consider the auxiliary function

(7.3)

f(\lambda)\coloneqq E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})),

with
\vec{v}_{T}
given by (
7.2
).
It follows from Lemma
2.10
that there exist constants
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
depending only on
n
such that

(7.4)

f(\lambda_{1})<nM\qquad f(\lambda_{2})>nM.

We claim that for
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
we have

(7.5)

f^{\prime}(\lambda)\gtrsim\rho(\vec{a}_{T}).

Indeed, we have
\partial_{\lambda}v_{T,k}=\lambda^{-1}v_{T,k}
, hence

(7.6)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})),\partial_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\Big\langle\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{T},\vec{v}_{T})),\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}v_{T,k}\partial_{v}\boldsymbol{H}(a_{T,k},v_{T,k})\Big\rangle,

and it suffices to use (
2.67
) and (
2.30
).
We conclude that there exists unique
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
such that
f(\lambda)=nM
.
Moreover,
\lambda
is continuous with respect to
\vec{a}_{T}
.

Step 3.
(Definition and continuity of the exit time.)
Note that
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\leq ne^{-L_{1}}
if
y_{k}(t)\geq L_{1}
for all
k
.
We claim that there exists
c_{0}>0
depending only on
n
such that

(7.7)

\widetilde{\rho}\,^{\prime}(t)\leq{-}c_{0}\widetilde{\rho}(t)^{\frac{3}{2}}\qquad\text{for all }t\in[T_{0},T].

Let
\beta
be defined as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
.
The same argument shows that
\beta(t)
is decreasing for
t\in[T_{0},T]
if
c_{0}
is small enough.

We claim that
\beta(T)>0
, which will imply that
\beta(t)>0
for all
t\in[T_{0},T]
.
The proof of (
6.32
) then applies without changes.
By the definition of
\beta
, it suffices to verify that

(7.8)

q_{k}(T)\gtrsim\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{T})}\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}.

But from (
4.51
) we obtain
|q_{k}(T)-(v_{T,k+1}-v_{T,k})|\lesssim\rho(\vec{a}_{T})
,
which implies (
7.8
) since
v_{T,k+1}-v_{T,k}=\lambda\sqrt{\rho(\vec{a}_{T})}
and
\lambda\simeq 1
.

Let
T_{1}\in[T_{0},T]
be the minimal time such that
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\leq 2ne^{-L_{1}}

for all
t\in[T_{1},T]
. Suppose that the last inequality holds for all
t\in[T_{0},T]
.
Then in particular
\widetilde{\rho}(T_{0})\leq 2ne^{-L_{1}}
,
and Lemma
4.2
yields
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(T_{0}))<\eta_{0}
if
L_{1}
is large enough,
thus
T_{0}=0
. In this case, we set
T_{1}\coloneqq 0
as well.

We claim that, for
T
fixed,
T_{1}=T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T})
is a continuous function.
Let
\vec{a}_{T,m}\to\vec{a}_{T}
and consider the corresponding sequence of solutions constructed above.

Assume first that
T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T})>0
, thus
\widetilde{\rho}(T_{1})=2ne^{-L_{1}}
.
Let
\epsilon>0
. Since
\widetilde{\rho}
is strictly decreasing by (
7.7
),
we have
\widetilde{\rho}(t)<2ne^{-L_{1}}
for all
t\in[T_{1}+\epsilon,T]
.
By the continuity of the flow, the same inequality holds for the
m
-th solution of the sequence if
m
is large enough,
hence
T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T,m})\leq T_{1}+\epsilon
if
m
is large enough.
Similarly,
\widetilde{\rho}(T_{1}-\epsilon)>2ne^{-L_{1}}
, implying
T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T,m})\geq T_{1}-\epsilon
for
m
large enough.

Now assume that
T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T})=T_{0}(\vec{a}_{T})=0
, thus
\widetilde{\rho}(0)\leq 2ne^{-L_{1}}
.
Since
\widetilde{\rho}
is strictly decreasing, for any
\epsilon>0
we have
\widetilde{\rho}(t)<2ne^{-L_{1}}
for all
t\in[\epsilon,T]
, and again the continuity of the flow yields
T_{1}(\vec{a}_{T,m})\leq\epsilon
for
m
large enough.

Step 4.
(Application of the Poincaré–Miranda theorem.)
We set

(7.9)

\vec{\Psi}(\vec{y}_{T})\coloneqq\vec{y}(T_{1}).

The preceding step together with the continuity of the flow imply that
\vec{\Psi}
is continuous.
Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
be such that
y_{T,k}=L_{2}
.
If
L_{2}
is sufficiently large, then
\Psi_{k}(\vec{y}_{T})\geq y_{0,k}

(it suffices to integrate in time the bound
|y_{k}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim\sqrt{\widetilde{\rho}(t)}\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}L_{1}}
).

Assume now that
y_{T,k}=L_{1}
, thus
\widetilde{\rho}(T)\geq e^{-L_{1}}
.
By (
7.7
), we have

(7.10)

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}}\big(\widetilde{\rho}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\gtrsim 1,\qquad\text{for all }t\in[T_{1},T].

Since
\widetilde{\rho}(T_{1})\leq 2ne^{-L_{1}}
, we obtain

(7.11)

T-T_{1}\lesssim e^{L_{1}/2}\big(1-(2n)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big).

But we also have
|y_{k}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}L_{1}}
for all
t\in[T_{1},T]
,
hence
|y_{k}(T_{1})-y_{k}(T)|\lesssim 1
, and it suffices to let
L_{1}=L-C
for
C
sufficiently large.

By Theorem
7.1
, there exists
\vec{y}_{T}\in[L_{1},L_{2}]^{n-1}
such that
\vec{\Psi}(\vec{y}_{T})=\vec{y}_{0}
.
In particular,
\widetilde{\rho}(T_{1})\lesssim e^{-L}=e^{-C}e^{-L_{1}}\ll 2ne^{-L_{1}}
if
C
is large.
We thus have
T_{0}=T_{1}=0
and
\vec{y}(0)=\vec{y}_{0}
.

Step 5.
(Estimates on the solution.)
By (
7.10
), we have

(7.12)

\widetilde{\rho}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\widetilde{\rho}(0)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gtrsim t,\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,T].

Since
\widetilde{\rho}(0)\lesssim e^{-L}
, we obtain
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\lesssim(e^{L}+t^{2})^{-1}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Let
T_{m}
be an increasing sequence tending to
\infty
and let

(7.13)

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m}(t),\vec{v}_{m}(t))+\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(t)

be the solution given by Lemma
7.3
for
T=T_{m}
.
After extraction of a subsequence, we can assume that
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(0)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{g}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}

and, using the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem,
(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})\to(\vec{a},\vec{v})
locally uniformly.
Let
\vec{v}_{0}\coloneqq\vec{v}(0)
and let
\boldsymbol{\phi}
be the solution of (
1.10
) such that
\boldsymbol{\phi}(0)=\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{0},\vec{v}_{0})+\boldsymbol{g}_{0}
.
We verify that
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is the desired kink cluster.

For all
m
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

(7.14)

\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{m,k}(0),v_{m,k}(0)),\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(0)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{m,k}(0),v_{m,k}(0)),\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(0)\rangle=0.

Passing to the weak limit, we obtain

(7.15)

\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(a_{0,k},v_{0,k}),\boldsymbol{g}_{0}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}(a_{0,k},v_{0,k}),\boldsymbol{g}_{0}\rangle=0,

hence
\vec{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(0))=\vec{a}_{0}
.

Fix
t\geq 0
. Since
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(0)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\phi}(0)
, Proposition
3.2

implies
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)
, hence

(7.16)

\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(t)=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m}(t),\vec{v}_{m})\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t)).

From (
4.32
) and Lemma
7.3

2
, we obtain

(7.17)

\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}(t)\|^{2}\leq C_{0}/(e^{L}+t^{2})\qquad\text{for all }m,

hence, by the weak compactness of closed balls in separable Hilbert spaces,

(7.18)

\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)-\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}(t),\vec{v}(t))\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\leq C_{0}/(e^{L}+t^{2}).

We also have
\rho(\vec{a}(t))=\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho(\vec{a}_{m}(t))\leq C_{0}/(e^{L}+t^{2})
,
yielding the required bound on
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(t))
by Remark
4.5
.
∎

8.
Kink clusters as profiles of kink collapse

In this final section, we prove Theorem
4
.
In the proof, we will need the following “Fatou property”.

Lemma 8.1
.

There exist
\eta_{0},y_{0}>0
such that the following holds. Let
\vec{a}_{m}=(a_{m,1},\ldots,a_{m,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\vec{v}_{m}=(v_{m,1},\ldots,v_{m,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

for all
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
be such that
a_{m,k+1}-a_{m,k}\geq y_{0}
for all
m
and
k
, and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\vec{a}_{m}=\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\lim_{m\to\infty}\vec{v}_{m}=\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}
,
\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq\eta_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}
satisfy the orthogonality conditions (
4.3
)
with
(\vec{a},\vec{v},\boldsymbol{g})
replaced by
(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m},\boldsymbol{g}_{m})
, and assume that
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E}
. Then

(8.1)

E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})+\boldsymbol{g}_{m}),

and
E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})=\lim_{m\to\infty}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a}_{m},\vec{v}_{m})+\boldsymbol{g}_{m})
if and only if
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\to\boldsymbol{g}
strongly in
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi\subset\mathcal{E}
be the subspace of codimension
2n
defined by (
4.3
).
Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{m}
be the orthogonal projection of
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}
on
\Pi
.
Then
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\|_{\mathcal{E}}=0
, hence we are reduced to the situation where
\vec{a}_{m}=\vec{a}
and
\vec{v}_{m}=\vec{v}
for all
m
.
To simplify the notation, we write
\boldsymbol{g}_{m}
instead of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}_{m}
.

We have the Taylor expansion around
\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}
:

(8.2)

\displaystyle E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}_{m})

\displaystyle=E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})+\langle\mathrm{D}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g}),\boldsymbol{g}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{D}^{2}E(\boldsymbol{H}(\vec{a},\vec{v})+\boldsymbol{g})(\boldsymbol{g}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}),\boldsymbol{g}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}\rangle+O(\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{3}).

The second term of the right hand side converges to 0 by assumption.
The second line is
\gtrsim\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}-\boldsymbol{g}\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}
, by the coercivity estimate in Lemma
2.13
and smallness of
\|\boldsymbol{g}_{m}\|_{\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Let
\vec{a}_{m}:[0,T_{m}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\vec{v}_{m}:[0,T_{m}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the modulation parameters corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}
.
Let
C_{1}
be the constant in (
4.31
).
Since
\lim_{m\to\infty}E(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m})=nM
,
there exists a sequence
\widetilde{T}_{m}\in[0,T_{m}]

so that
{\bf d}(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m}(t))\geq C_{1}(E(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{m})-nM)
for all
m

and
